```yaml
observation_id: obs_symposium_0001
source_work: Symposium
stephanus_span: 172a-173b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/symposium.txt
  stephanus_span: 172a-173b
  start_marker: 172a
  end_marker: 173b
  start_char: 5
  end_char: 2240
  text_sha256: bc7cfd18a5ce061a7dabef9a982bb82463f76b2582aac5ef08daf7d280e5c17f
greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: frame_depth
feature_id: feature_candidate_325
feature_label: reported_dialogue_frame
observation: "The Symposium opens with a multi-layered narrative frame. An unnamed companion asks Apollodorus to recount the symposium (172a-172b). Apollodorus was not present himself but heard the account from Aristodemus, who attended (173b). Apollodorus also cross-checked some details with Socrates, who confirmed Aristodemus's account (173b). The dialogue is thus at least three removes from the original event: symposium participants to Aristodemus to Apollodorus to unnamed companion."
textual_basis: "At 172a-172b, the companion asks Apollodorus: 'tell me about the gathering at Agathon's... about the erotic speeches, what they were.' At 173b, Apollodorus states he heard it from Aristodemus, not Socrates directly: 'No, by Zeus... it was the same man who told Phoenix. A certain Aristodemus, of Cydathenaeum... he had been present at the gathering.' He adds: 'Not that I haven't also questioned Socrates about some things I heard from that man, and he agreed it was just as he narrated.' This establishes the chain of transmission explicitly within the text."
limits: "This observation records the explicit reporting chain stated in the text. It does not assess whether the frame is reliable, ironic, or designed to create distance from the events. It does not claim that every layer adds distortion."
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: "At 173b, Apollodorus names his source: a certain Aristodemus of Cydathenaeum, small, always barefoot; he had been present at the gathering, being a lover of Socrates among the foremost of those at that time, as it seems to me. The physical markers small and always barefoot are presented as identifying traits, alongside the deme and the Socratic relationship."
limits: "This observation notes the traits the text supplies for Aristodemus. It does not interpret the significance of the barefoot detail (e.g., as emulation of Socrates, who also went barefoot), nor assess Aristodemus's reliability as a source."

  - "\u1f00\u03b8\u03bb\u03b9\u03ce\u03c4\u03b5\u03c1\u03bf\u03c2"
  - "\u03c0\u03ac\u03bd\u03c4\u03b1 \u03bc\u1fb6\u03bb\u03bb\u03bf\u03bd \u03c0\u03c1\u03ac\u03c4\u03c4\u03b5\u03b9\u03bd \u1f22 \u03c6\u03b9\u03bb\u03bf\u03c3\u03bf\u03c6\u03b5\u1fd6\u03bd"
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: narrator_self_disclosure
feature_id: feature_candidate_1203
feature_label: narrator_self_disclosure
observation: "At 172c-173a, Apollodorus discloses his own philosophical biography in response to Glaucon's assumption that the symposium was recent. He reports that he has been following Socrates daily for under three years (172c) and that before this he ran about aimlessly, was more miserable than anyone, and believed one should do everything except philosophize. He adds that Glaucon is currently in the same condition."
textual_basis: "At 172c Apollodorus says he has been spending time with Socrates and making it his practice each day to know whatever he says or does, for not yet three years. At 173a he continues: before that, running about wherever he happened to be, thinking he was doing something, he was more miserable than anyone - no less than Glaucon is now - thinking one should do everything rather than philosophize. The contrast is drawn between aimless pre-philosophical misery and disciplined daily attention to Socrates."
limits: "This observation records the biographical self-disclosure Apollodorus offers. It does not assess whether this disclosure is sincere, ironic, or a rhetorical strategy to establish narratorial authority. It does not claim that this is Plato's own view of philosophy's benefit."
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "The companion characterizes Apollodorus as always the same: he always badmouths himself and others, thinks everyone miserable except Socrates, and is called malakos (soft) yet in speech is wild and harsh to everyone except Socrates. The companion expresses bafflement at how Apollodorus acquired the epithet malakos given his aggressive speech."
textual_basis: "At 173d, the companion says: 'You are always the same, Apollodorus; always you badmouth yourself and others, and you seem to me to think everyone utterly miserable except Socrates, starting with yourself. And whence you ever got this epithet, being called malakos, I do not know; for in speech you are always like this, wild toward yourself and others except Socrates.' The epithet malakos (soft) is explicitly marked as a nickname whose origin puzzles the companion given Apollodorus's verbal aggressiveness (agriaineis)."
limits: "This observation records the character portrait and nickname the companion provides. It does not assess whether the companion's description is reliable, nor whether the tension between the nickname malakos and Apollodorus's aggressive speech has thematic significance."
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: "In the embedded narration starting at 174a, Aristodemus reports that he met Socrates washed (leloumenon) and wearing sandals (tas blautas hypodedemenon), 'which that man rarely did' (ha ekeinos oligakis epoiei). Socrates says he beautified himself 'so that a beautiful man might go to a beautiful man' (hina kalos para kalon iō), having promised to attend Agathon's dinner after avoiding the victory-celebration crowd the previous day."

  - ἄκλητος

feature_label: poetic_citation_as_argument
observation: "Socrates proposes to corrupt the proverb about Agathon by changing it, then supports his revision with a Homeric exegesis about Agamemnon and Menelaus. Aristodemus responds by applying the Homeric version to himself, reversing Socrates's playful frame: where Socrates suggests the good go spontaneously to Agathon, Aristodemus casts himself as the Homeric lesser man going uninvited to a wise man's feast."
textual_basis: "At 174b Socrates says 'follow, so that we may corrupt (diaphtheirōmen) the proverb by changing it (metabalontes), that the good go spontaneously to Agathon's feasts.' He then cites Homer: Agamemnon as exceptionally good in war and Menelaus as 'soft spearman' (malthakon aichmētēn), noting that Agamemnon made Menelaus come uninvited (aklēton), a lesser man to a better man's feast (174c). Aristodemus replies that perhaps he risks going not as Socrates says but Homerically — a lowly man (phaulos) going uninvited to a wise man's feast — and asks Socrates to defend him (174c)."
limits: "This observation records the proverb manipulation and Homeric citation as a textual event. It does not assess whether this is ironic, whether it foreshadows themes in the speeches to follow, or whether the Homeric citation is accurate."
review_status: accepted
```

feature_label: socratic_habit_named_by_associate
observation: "At 175a-b, a slave reports that Socrates is standing in the neighbor's porch and refuses to enter when called. Agathon finds this strange and insists the slave call him again. Aristodemus, Socrates's associate, intervenes to explain that Socrates has this as a habit. He adds that Socrates sometimes stands apart wherever he happens to be and will come soon, telling them not to disturb him. An insider explains and normalizes a behavior that appears bizarre to the host."
textual_basis: "At 175a, the slave reports: this Socrates has withdrawn and is standing in the neighbor's porch, and though I call him, he is unwilling to come in. Agathon at 175a says: what you say is strange (atopon); call him and don't let him go. At 175b, Aristodemus (reported through Apollodorus) says: by no means, but let him be; for he has this habit. Sometimes he stands apart wherever he happens to be. He will come soon, I think. So don't disturb him, but let him be. Agathon then defers: we must do so, if it seems right to you."
limits: "This observation records that an associate names and explains Socrates's behavior as a known habit, and that the host defers to this explanation. It does not assess whether Aristodemus's explanation is correct or whether the habit has philosophical significance. It does not claim that Agathon's deference signals anything beyond politeness."
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "At 175c, when Socrates finally enters after standing in the porch, Agathon, who happens to be reclining last and alone, invites Socrates to recline beside him: come here, recline beside me, so that by touching you I may also enjoy the wisdom that came to you in the porch. The host's seating arrangement explicitly links physical contact with access to wisdom."
textual_basis: "At 175c, after the diners have begun eating without Socrates and he arrives roughly mid-meal, Agathon, who is reclining last and alone, says: here, Socrates, recline beside me, so that by touching you I may also enjoy the wisdom that came to you in the porch. The verb for touching is physical; the object is the wisdom; the expected mechanism is that physical proximity to Socrates transfers the intellectual benefit Socrates gained while standing outside."
limits: "This observation records the seating invitation and its stated rationale. It does not assess whether Agathon is being ironic, whether the touch-wisdom link is meant seriously, or whether this foreshadows Alcibiades's later entrance and seating. It does not interpret the philosophical valence of sophou in Agathon's mouth."

```yaml
observation_id: obs_symposium_0010
source_work: Symposium
stephanus_span: 175d-175e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/symposium.txt
  stephanus_span: 175d-175e
  start_marker: 175d
  end_marker: 175e
  start_char: 7031
  end_char: 7963
  text_sha256: e175c1f3cdc84e12c13e8c1c8e469b500a51863e6467ca11f5b8f623d56fe4ef
greek_terms:
  - φαύλη
  - ἀμφισβητήσιμος
  - ὄναρ
  - πληρεστέρας
  - κενώτερον
  - ἐρίου
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: craft_analogy
feature_id: feature_candidate_145
feature_label: analogy_proposed_as_counterfactual
observation: "At 175d-175e, Socrates responds to Agathon's invitation to recline beside him by proposing an analogy: if wisdom were such as to flow by contact from the fuller to the emptier, like water through wool from a fuller cup to an emptier one. The analogy is framed counterfactually (ei toiouton eiē hē sophia, 'if wisdom were such'), and Socrates immediately contrasts his own wisdom as paltry (phaulē) and contestable as a dream (amphisbētēsimos hōsper onar ousa) with Agathon's, which shone forth brilliantly before more than 30,000 Greek witnesses. The analogy is proposed not as a claim about how wisdom actually works but as a hypothetical whose falsity is implied by the contrast that follows."
textual_basis: "At 175d, after Agathon invites Socrates to recline beside him to enjoy his wisdom by touch, Socrates says: 'It would be well, Agathon, if wisdom were such as to flow from the fuller to the emptier of us, if we touch each other, just as the water in cups flows through wool from the fuller to the emptier one.' The counterfactual ei ... eiē construction is followed immediately at 175e by the contrast: 'For mine would be paltry, or even contestable as a dream, while yours is bright and has much increase, which from you, young as you are, shone forth so intensely and became manifest the other day before more than 30,000 Greek witnesses.' The analogy's domain mapping (wisdom to water, contact to wool-mediated flow) is presented only to be undermined by the self-deprecating contrast."
limits: "This observation records the analogy's counterfactual framing and the self-other contrast. It does not assess whether Socrates is sincere in his self-deprecation or whether the contrast is ironic. It does not evaluate whether the water-through-wool image alludes to specific Greek physical theories."
review_status: accepted
```

  - ὑβριστής
  - διαδικασόμεθα
  - δικαστῇ
  - Διονύσῳ
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: dramatic_case_setup
feature_id: feature_candidate_1206
feature_label: symposium_quasi_judicial_frame
observation: "At 175e, Agathon calls Socrates a hybristēs (an insolent or outrageously arrogant person) in response to Socrates's self-deprecating contrast between their wisdoms. Agathon then proposes that he and Socrates will litigate (diadikasometha) the matter of wisdom a little later, using Dionysus as judge (dikastēi chrōmenoi tōi Dionysōi). The intellectual contest of the symposium is explicitly framed as a legal dispute with a divine adjudicator, and the god of wine and theater is named as the presiding judge."
textual_basis: "At 175e, immediately after Socrates contrasts his paltry wisdom with Agathon's brilliant public display, the text reads: 'You are a hybristēs, Socrates,' said Agathon. 'And we will litigate these matters about wisdom a little later, you and I, using Dionysus as judge. But now turn first to dinner.' The verb diadikasometha is a legal term for contesting a claim; dikastēs is a juror or judge; Dionysus is explicitly named as the adjudicator. The framing suspends the dispute rather than resolving it and defers it to a later phase of the symposium."
limits: "This observation records the hybristēs charge and the proposed legal framing with Dionysus as judge. It does not assess whether Agathon is joking, whether the hybristēs charge is justified, or whether the Dionysus-as-judge framing prefigures the symposium's dramatic or thematic arc. It does not claim that the later speeches constitute the trial Agathon proposes."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_symposium_0012
source_work: Symposium
stephanus_span: 176a-176c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/symposium.txt
  stephanus_span: 176a-176c
  start_marker: 176a
  end_marker: 176c
  start_char: 7963
  end_char: 9271
  text_sha256: f850d3ee5214749b056be69d752fd06600fee931c6a5f3f85cc9fd8c4e33b080
greek_terms:
  - βεβαπτισμένων
  - Ἕρμαιον
  - ἐρρῶσθαι πίνειν
  - ἐξαιρῶ λόγου
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: turn_geometry
feature_id: feature_candidate_720
feature_label: explicit_procedure_control
observation: "At 176a-176c, after the dinner and libations, Pausanias opens the drinking phase by asking how they can drink most easily, citing his hangover from the previous day. Aristophanes agrees, calling himself one of the 'baptized' from yesterday. Eryximachus then polls Agathon about his drinking strength; when Agathon also declines, Eryximachus declares this a windfall (Hermaion) for the weak drinkers present, exempts Socrates from the discussion (Sōkratē d' exairō logou) on the grounds that Socrates is capable either way, and consolidates procedural control by declaring that no one present is eager to drink heavily. He then positions himself to speak about the nature of drunkenness."
textual_basis: "At 176a, Pausanias says: 'I am truly in a very bad way from yesterday's drinking and need some recovery — and I think most of you too, for you were present yesterday — so consider how we might drink most easily.' At 176b, Aristophanes says: 'I too am one of those baptized yesterday.' Eryximachus then asks: 'How does Agathon stand with respect to being strong for drinking?' Agathon answers: 'Not at all; I'm not strong either.' At 176c, Eryximachus says: 'It would be a windfall for us, it seems, for me and Aristodemus and Phaedrus and these others, if you who are the strongest drinkers have now given up. For we are always weak. I exempt Socrates from the discussion, for he is capable of both, so he will suffice whichever we do. Since then it seems to me that no one present is eager to drink much wine...' The sequence moves from complaint to polling to consolidation to Eryximachus taking the floor to propose an alternative."
limits: "This observation records the procedural sequence by which Eryximachus consolidates control of the drinking format. It does not assess whether Eryximachus's proposal (which leads to the speech competition) is pre-arranged, whether the others' hangover claims are genuine, or whether the exemption of Socrates carries ironic weight."
review_status: accepted
```

feature_label: explicit_procedure_control
observation: "At 176e, Eryximachus proposes dismissing the aulos-girl who has just entered and replacing her musical entertainment with competitive speeches among the men. The symposium pivots from a conventional drinking party with hired female entertainment to an all-male logoi competition. Eryximachus explicitly frames this as the next procedural decision after the drinking norms have been settled."
textual_basis: "At 176e, after the group agrees to drink moderately without compulsion, Eryximachus says: 'I propose to dismiss the aulos-girl who just entered — let her play for herself or for the women indoors if she wishes — and for us to spend today with each other through speeches (dia logōn allēlois syneinai). And through what kind of speeches, if you wish, I am willing to propose.' The aulos-girl is sent away (chairein ean), and logoi are proposed as the replacement activity for the male company. Eryximachus presents this immediately after the drinking rules are settled, marking it as the next procedural step."
limits: "This observation records the procedural decision to replace musical entertainment with logoi. It does not assess the gender dynamics of sending the female performer to the women's quarters, nor whether Eryximachus's proposal was pre-planned. It does not claim that this pivot signals a philosophical elevation of discourse over sensory entertainment."
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "At 177a-177c, Eryximachus provides a dramatic rationale for the speech topic by reporting Phaedrus's recurring complaint that no poet or prose-writer has ever composed an encomium to Eros. He introduces the complaint with a Euripides citation: 'the story is not mine but Phaedrus's here' (from Melanippe). Phaedrus's complaint catalogs encomia to other gods (hymns and paeans), to Heracles (by Prodicus), and even to salt (in a book by a wise man), contrasting this abundance with the total neglect of Eros. Eryximachus endorses the complaint and frames the proposed speeches as an eranos (contribution-feast) for Phaedrus and an adornment of the neglected god."
textual_basis: "At 177a, Eryximachus says: 'The beginning of my speech is according to Euripides' Melanippe: for the story is not mine, but Phaedrus's here, which I am about to tell.' He then reports Phaedrus's complaint in direct speech (177a-177c): it is terrible (ou deinon) that other gods have hymns and paeans from poets, while Eros, such a great god, has never had a single encomium from any poet. Even prose-writers like Prodicus praise Heracles, and a wise man wrote a book praising salt — yet no human has dared to hymn Eros worthily. At 177c, Eryximachus says: 'Phaedrus seems to me to speak well. I therefore desire at once to contribute an eranos to him and to do him a favor, and at the same time it seems fitting for us here present to adorn the god.' The complaint functions as the dramatic justification for why the speeches will be encomia to Eros."
limits: "This observation records the reported complaint as the text's internal rationale for the speech topic. It does not assess whether Phaedrus's complaint is factually accurate about the Greek literary record, whether Eryximachus accurately reports Phaedrus, or whether the salt-encomium reference is a joke. It does not interpret the Euripides citation as a claim about tragic authority."
review_status: accepted
```

english_gloss: ""
feature_family: irony_marker
feature_id: feature_candidate_084
feature_label: knowledge_disavowal
observation: "At 177d, Socrates responds to Eryximachus's proposal that each person deliver an encomium to Eros. He states that no one will oppose the plan — not himself, who claims to know nothing except erotics. The claim has a double structure: it disavows knowledge in general while simultaneously asserting a single domain of expertise, and that domain is precisely the topic of the proposed speeches. The claim that he knows only erotics both explains why he cannot object to the plan and positions him as uniquely qualified among the speakers."
textual_basis: "At 177d, after Eryximachus proposes the encomium competition, Socrates says: 'No one, Eryximachus, will vote against you. For neither would I refuse, who claim to know nothing except erotics (hos ouden phēmi allo epistasthai ē ta erōtika), nor surely Agathon and Pausanias...' The verb epistasthai indicates knowledge or understanding; the negation ouden allo ('nothing else') limits his knowledge-claims to a single domain; the exception ta erōtika is the very subject of the proposed speeches. The claim serves simultaneously as a reason for compliance and an assertion of distinctive competence."
limits: "This observation records the structure of the knowledge-claim at 177d. It does not assess whether Socrates is being ironic, whether the claim is consistent with other Socratic disavowals elsewhere, or whether 'knowing erotics' is meant to be understood as philosophical expertise or something else. It does not claim that this is a sincere assertion of competence."
review_status: accepted
```

Neither did Aristodemus fully remember everything each person said, nor did Apollodorus himself remember everything Aristodemus said. He will relay only what seemed to him especially worthy of remembrance (axiomnemoneuton) from each speech. The disclaimer qualifies both layers of the narrative transmission chain simultaneously."
textual_basis: "At 178a, after the company agrees to the encomium plan and Phaedrus is instructed to begin, the text reads: panton men oun ha hekastos eipen, oute pany ho Aristodemos ememnato out' au ego ha ekeinos elege panta; ha de malista kai hon edoxe moi axiomnemoneuton, touton hymin ero hekastou ton logon (178a). The two clauses are parallel: Aristodemus did not remember all, and Apollodorus did not remember all that Aristodemus said. The positive clause limits the report to what seemed most memorable, not what was most accurate or complete."
limits: "This observation records the explicit memory disclaimer at the transition to the speeches. It does not assess whether the disclaimer is a literary device, whether it undermines or enhances narratorial credibility, or whether it selectively shapes which speeches are reported in full."

feature_label: mythic_origin_taxonomy
observation: "Phaedrus opens his encomium at 178a-178c by arguing that Eros is the oldest (presbytaton) of the gods. His proof (tekmerion) is the absence of parents: no one, layman or poet, names Eros's parents (178a-178b). He then cites three poetic-theological authorities as convergent witnesses: Hesiod (Chaos, then broad-breasted Earth, and Eros), Acusilaus (agrees with Hesiod: after Chaos, Earth and Eros), and Parmenides (Eros was devised first of all gods). The conclusion draws on multiple sources: from many places it is agreed (pollachothen homologeitai) that Eros is among the oldest. The argument uses cosmogonic genealogy and poetic citation as evidence within an encomiastic speech."
textual_basis: "At 178a, Phaedrus says Eros is a great god, wondrous among humans and gods, not least concerning his genesis (kata ten genesin). At 178a-178b: to gar en tois presbytaton einai ton theon timion — the oldest is honored — and the proof (tekmerion): of Eros there are no parents, nor are they spoken of by anyone, layman or poet (178b). Hesiod is cited: first Chaos, then broad-breasted Earth and Eros (178b, Theogony). Acusilaus agrees: after Chaos these two, Earth and Eros (178b). Parmenides: first of all gods she devised Eros (178b, a fragment). The conclusion at 178c: pollachothen homologeitai ho Eros en tois presbytatos einai."
limits: "This observation records Phaedrus's argument structure and the poetic authorities he invokes. It does not assess whether the Hesiod and Parmenides citations are textually accurate, whether Phaedrus's reasoning from absent parents to great antiquity is valid, or whether the argument is meant to be taken seriously or ironically."
review_status: accepted
```

greek_terms:
  - "\u03b1\u1f30\u03c3\u03c7\u03cd\u03bd\u03b7\u03bd"
  - "\u03c6\u03b9\u03bb\u03bf\u03c4\u03b9\u03bc\u03af\u03b1\u03bd"
  - "\u03c0\u03b1\u03b9\u03b4\u03b9\u03ba\u1ff6\u03bd"
  - "\u1f10\u03c1\u03b1\u03c3\u03c4\u1ff6\u03bd"
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: erotic_argument
feature_id: feature_candidate_1210
feature_label: eros_as_civic_ordering_force
observation: "At 178c-178e, Phaedrus argues that Eros, as the oldest god, is the cause of the greatest goods for humans. He identifies the specific good: shame at shameful things (aischynen) and love of honor (philotimian) at noble things. Neither family, nor honors, nor wealth can produce this as well as Eros does, and without it no city or individual can accomplish great and noble deeds (178c-178d). The psychological mechanism is concrete: a lover caught in a shameful act would suffer greater pain being seen by his beloved than by his father, companions, or anyone else (178d-178e). The same holds for the beloved, who feels especial shame before his lovers (178e). Phaedrus extends this to a civic thought-experiment: if there could be a city or army of lovers and beloveds, they would govern themselves best, abstaining from all shameful things and competing in honor toward each other (178e)."
textual_basis: "At 178c: presbytatos de on megiston agathon hemin aitios estin. At 178c-178d: the greatest good for a young man is a good lover and for a lover, a beloved; what humans need to live well through their whole life — neither family, nor honors, nor wealth can produce this as well as Eros does. What is this? At 178d: ten epi men tois aischrois aischynen, epi de tois kalois philotimian. At 178d: a man who loves, if he were caught doing something shameful or suffering something through cowardice without defending himself, would not be so pained being seen by his father, companions, or anyone else as by his beloved. Same for the beloved (178e): he especially feels shame before his lovers. At 178e: if there were some device (mechanē) so that a city or army of lovers and beloveds could come to be, they could not govern themselves better than by abstaining from all shameful things and competing in honor toward each other."
limits: "This observation records Phaedrus's argument that erotic shame and love of honor are the civic goods Eros provides, and the psychological mechanism by which the beloved's gaze enforces virtue. It does not assess whether the argument is valid, whether the military thought-experiment alludes to the Theban Sacred Band, or whether Phaedrus's claims about the lover-beloved relationship are normative rather than descriptive."
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "At 179a-179b, Phaedrus claims that what Homer described as a god breathing menos (might) into some heroes is exactly what Eros provides to lovers, coming from himself. Eros is explicitly substituted for the Homeric divine agent, and the lovers become the analogues of Homeric heroes. The claim caps Phaedrus's argument that no one is so base that Eros cannot make him inspired (entheon) toward virtue, making him like the best by nature."
textual_basis: "At 179a-179b: Phaedrus argues that no one is so base that Eros would not make him inspired (entheon) toward virtue, so as to be like the best by nature. Then: 'and simply, what Homer said, that the god breathed might (menos empneusai) into some of the heroes, this Eros provides to lovers, coming from himself (par' hautou).' The word entheon (having god within) bridges the two claims: Eros makes lovers god-filled, and the Homeric mechanism of divine menos-inspiration is reassigned to Eros as its source."
limits: "This observation records the explicit substitution of Eros for Homeric divine agency. It does not assess whether Phaedrus is using Homer accurately, whether the reassignment is theologically coherent, or whether the claim is meant to be taken seriously or as rhetorical hyperbole. It does not interpret the philosophical significance of par' hautou."
review_status: accepted
```

greek_terms:
  - ὑπεραποθνῄσκειν
  - Ἄλκηστις
  - Ὀρφέα
  - ἀτελῆ
  - φάσμα
  - Ἀχιλλέα
  - ἐπαποθανεῖν
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: myth_demarcation
feature_id: feature_candidate_155
feature_label: triadic_mythic_exemplum_chain_with_ranking
observation: "At 179b-180a, Phaedrus deploys a chain of three mythic exempla to prove that lovers alone are willing to die for one another, and that the gods honor this the most. The three are arranged in an ascending rank: Alcestis as positive exemplar (died for her husband, gods released her soul from Hades, 179b-179d); Orpheus as negative exemplar (sent away from Hades incomplete with only a phantom, because he was soft and contrived to enter alive rather than die, gods made his death come by women, 179d-179e); Achilles as superlative exemplar (honored and sent to the Isles of the Blessed because he chose to die for his lover Patroclus, dying not only for him but after him, 179e-180a). The chain is explicitly comparative: each exemplum is introduced with a contrast marker against the previous one."
textual_basis: "At 179b, Phaedrus states the thesis: only lovers are willing to die for another (hyperapothneskein), not only men but also women. Alcestis is introduced as sufficient witness (hikane martyria) at 179b-179c: she alone was willing to die for her husband despite his parents being alive; her philia through eros so surpassed them that she made them seem alien. The gods released her soul, admiring her deed (179c-179d). Orpheus is contrasted: the gods sent him away incomplete (atele) from Hades, showing only a phantom (phasma), because he seemed soft (malthakizesthai), being a kithara-player, not daring to die for eros like Alcestis but contriving to enter Hades alive (179d). Punishment: death by women (179d-179e). Achilles is contrasted against Orpheus: the gods honored him and sent him to the Isles of the Blessed because, knowing from his mother that killing Hector meant his death while not doing so meant dying old at home, he chose to help his lover Patroclus, dying not only for him (hyperapothanein) but also after him (epapothanein) when he was already dead (179e-180a). The gods exceedingly admired him because he valued his lover so highly (180a)."
limits: "This observation records the structure of the exemplum chain and its explicit contrasts. It does not assess whether Phaedrus's versions of the Alcestis, Orpheus, and Achilles myths are accurate to their traditional forms, whether the ranking is logically coherent, or whether the argument from mythic examples is rhetorically or philosophically sound."
review_status: accepted
```

Phaedrus argues that Achilles was the beloved (paidika), not the lover, citing three physical markers from Homeric evidence: Achilles was more beautiful (kallion) than Patroclus and all heroes, beardless (ageneios), and much younger (neoteros). The correction reverses the erotic roles in the Achilles-Patroclus relationship and uses Homer as the authoritative textual source against Aeschylean tragedy."
textual_basis: "At 180a, immediately after the Achilles exemplum, Phaedrus says: 'Aeschylus talks nonsense (phluarei) saying Achilles was the lover of Patroclus, who was more beautiful not only than Patroclus but at once also than all the heroes, and still beardless, then much younger, as Homer says.' The verb phluarei is dismissive; the correction is grounded in three physical attributes (beauty, beardlessness, youth) cited as Homeric evidence. The implicit reasoning is that the more beautiful and younger partner must be the beloved, not the lover. The span ends with Phaedrus concluding that the gods honor most the virtue concerning eros, and the beloved (who values the lover) more than the lover (who is possessed by the god)."
limits: "This observation records the explicit correction of Aeschylus and the Homeric evidence cited. It does not assess whether Phaedrus's reading of Homer is accurate, whether the beauty-age criterion for determining lover-beloved roles was conventional in Athenian pederastic discourse, or whether the correction has dramatic significance beyond Phaedrus's speech."

observation: "Pausanias opens his speech at 180c-180d by objecting that the encomium topic was not well set up (ou kalos probeblesthai). The problem is that the instruction simply commanded them to praise Eros without qualification (haplos houtos). Pausanias argues this framing is defective because Eros is not one; if he were one, it would be fine, but since he is not one, it is more correct first to specify which Eros should be praised (180d). He frames his own speech as an attempt at correction (epanorthosasthai): first identify the Eros to be praised, then praise him worthily of the god. This is a participant-initiated revision of the competition's procedural terms after the first speech has already been delivered."
textual_basis: "At 180c, Pausanias says: 'It does not seem to me, Phaedrus, that the topic has been well set for us — to have been instructed so simply to praise Eros. For if Eros were one, it would be well, but as it is, he is not one. And since he is not one, it is more correct first to state in advance which one should be praised.' At 180d he continues: 'I therefore will try to correct this — first to indicate which Eros should be praised, then to praise him worthily of the god.' The verb probeblesthai has the sense of a proposal or motion being put forward; epanorthosasthai means to set right or correct. The objection is procedural: the initial framing was underspecified."
limits: "This observation records Pausanias's procedural objection and his announced plan to correct the speech topic. It does not assess whether his correction is justified, whether the initial under-specification of the topic was intentional or negligent, or whether Pausanias's speech itself satisfies his corrected framework. It does not claim that Pausanias's move is polemical against Phaedrus."

```

greek_terms:

  - "\u03c0\u03c1\u03b5\u03c3\u03b2\u03c5\u03c4\u03ad\u03c1\u03b1"
  - "\u1f00\u03bc\u03ae\u03c4\u03c9\u03c1"
  - "\u03c3\u03c5\u03bd\u03b5\u03c1\u03b3\u03cc\u03bd"
english_gloss: ""

feature_id: feature_candidate_158
feature_label: divine_genealogy_used_to_bifurcate_subject
observation: "At 180d-180e, Pausanias grounds his bifurcation of Eros in the existence of two Aphrodites. He states that everyone knows Aphrodite is not without Eros; if there were one Aphrodite, there would be one Eros; since there are two goddesses, there must necessarily be two Erotes. He differentiates the two Aphrodites by genealogy: the elder (presbytera) is motherless (ametor), daughter of Ouranos, called Ourania; the younger (neotera) is daughter of Zeus and Dione, called Pandemos. From this premise he derives that the Eros assisting the elder Aphrodite is correctly called Ouranios, and the one assisting the younger is Pandemos. The theological bifurcation is used to restructure the terms of the encomium: one must praise all gods, but should try to say what each has received as its province."
textual_basis: "At 180d: 'Everyone knows that Aphrodite is not without Eros. If she were one, Eros would be one; but since there are two, there must necessarily also be two Erotes. And how is the goddess not two? The one is surely older and motherless, daughter of Ouranos, whom we call Ourania; the other is younger, daughter of Zeus and Dione, whom we call Pandemos (180e). It is necessary that the Eros assisting the one be correctly called Pandemos, the other Ouranios (180e).' The argument moves from a shared premise (Aphrodite and Eros are inseparable) through a genealogy (Ourania vs Pandemos by parentage) to a necessary conclusion (two Erotes, each named after the Aphrodite he assists). The term synergon (helper, co-worker) describes the relationship between each Eros and his Aphrodite."
limits: "This observation records the genealogical derivation of two Erotes from two Aphrodites. It does not assess whether Pausanias's genealogy is original, whether the logical move from two Aphrodites to two Erotes is valid, or whether the Ourania-Pandemos distinction carries evaluative weight beyond its stated descriptive function. It does not claim that Pausanias's framework is more correct than Phaedrus's unitary treatment of Eros."
review_status: accepted
```

english_gloss: ""

feature_id: feature_candidate_1164
feature_label: criterion_for_evaluative_classification
observation: "At 180e-181a, Pausanias introduces a general principle: no action (praxis) is noble (kale) or shameful (aischra) in itself, but becomes so depending on how it is performed. He illustrates with the present symposium activities — drinking, singing, conversing — none of which is noble in itself; what matters is the manner of performance. He then applies this principle to Eros: not all Eros is noble or worthy of encomium, but only the Eros that prompts one to love nobly (181a). The principle serves to restrict the encomium's scope: it justifies praising Ouranios Eros while withholding praise from Pandemos Eros, whom Pausanias immediately begins to characterize as pursuing any chance object, loving women no less than boys, bodies more than souls, the most unintelligent partners, and looking only to accomplish the act (181a-181b)."
textual_basis: "At 180e-181a: 'Every action is like this: performed in itself, it is neither noble nor shameful. For instance, what we are doing now — drinking, singing, conversing — none of these is noble in itself, but in the action it turns out such as it is performed. Performed nobly and rightly it becomes noble; not rightly, shameful.' Pausanias then applies this to the dialogue's subject: 'So too loving and Eros are not all noble nor worthy of being encomiast, but only the one that prompts to love nobly' (181a). He then characterizes Pandemos Eros: 'The Eros of Pandemos Aphrodite is truly common (pandemos) and accomplishes whatever it happens upon; and this is the one whom base people (phauloi) love' (181a-181b). The base lovers are described through a series of markers: they love women no less than boys, bodies more than souls, the most unintelligent possible, aiming only at accomplishment, careless of whether it is done nobly or not (181b)."
limits: "This observation records the praxis-neutrality principle and its application to restrict the encomium's scope. It does not assess whether the principle is philosophically sound, whether the characterization of Pandemos Eros is fair or internally consistent, or whether Pausanias's distinction between the two Erotes maps onto a normative hierarchy. It does not claim that Pausanias's argument anticipates or is undermined by later speeches."
review_status: accepted
```

english_gloss: ""

feature_id: feature_candidate_1164
feature_label: criterion_for_evaluative_classification
observation: "At 181c-181e, Pausanias provides a concrete behavioral criterion to distinguish followers of Ouranian Eros from those of Pandemos Eros. The Ouranian Eros comes from the Aphrodite who participates only in the male, not the female, and is older and free from hubris (hybreos amoirou). Its inspired followers turn toward the male, loving what is naturally stronger and has more intelligence (noun mallon echon). The key diagnostic: one can recognize the purely Ouranian-inspired even within paiderastia itself, because they do not love boys but wait until the beloved begins to have intelligence (noun ischein), which approaches the time of first beard (geneiaskein, 181d). These lovers intend lifelong partnership; the base lovers deceive, seize the beloved in his foolishness as young, laugh, and run off to another (181d). Pausanias even proposes a law against loving boys (nomon einai me eran paidon, 181e) because the outcome (telos) of boys is unclear (adelon) regarding vice and virtue of both soul and body. The good impose this law on themselves voluntarily."
textual_basis: "At 181c, the Ouranian Aphrodite's traits are listed: she participates only in the male (ou metechouses theleos all' arrenos monon), is older, and is without hubris (hybreos amoirou). Her followers turn toward the male, loving the naturally stronger and more intelligent (to physei erromenesteron kai noun mallon echon agapontes). At 181d, the diagnostic within paiderastia: true Ouranian-inspired lovers do not love boys but wait until intelligence begins (epeidan ede archontai noun ischein), which approaches first beard (touto de plesiazei toi geneiaskein). They intend to spend their whole life together (ton bion hapanta synesomenoi kai koinei symbiosomenoi). The contrast: base lovers deceive, take the beloved in foolishness (en aphrosynei) as young, laugh, and run off to another (181d). At 181e: there ought to be a law not to love boys (chren de kai nomon einai me eran paidon), so that much zeal is not spent into something unclear (eis adelon); the boys' outcome (telos) is unclear as to vice and virtue of soul and body. The good impose this law on themselves voluntarily (hoi men oun agathoi ton nomon touton autoi hautois hekontes tithentai)."
limits: "This observation records the behavioral criterion Pausanias uses to distinguish the two erotic types. It does not assess whether this criterion is coherent, whether the age/intelligence threshold reflects Athenian norms or Pausanias's own preference, or whether Pausanias's proposal of a law is meant seriously or as rhetorical amplification. It does not claim that this distinction anticipates or is contradicted by later speeches in the Symposium."
review_status: accepted
```

english_gloss: ""

feature_id: feature_candidate_161
feature_label: erotic_customs_by_political_regime
observation: "At 182a-182b, Pausanias maps the normative status of granting favors to lovers across a comparative geography of Greek and non-Greek cities. He claims the custom (nomos) concerning eros is simple (haplos horistai) in other cities but complicated (poikilos) at Athens and Sparta (182a-182b). He identifies two regime-types: (1) Elis, Boeotia, and places where men are not clever at speaking (hou me sophoi legein): it is simply legislated as noble (kalon) to grant favors to lovers, because the inhabitants, being incapable of speech (adynatoi legein), would otherwise have trouble persuading the young (182b). (2) Ionia and many places under barbarians (hypo barbarois): it is considered shameful. Before this taxonomy, Pausanias also addresses the internal Athenian dispute at 182a: the base popular lovers have created the disgrace (oneidos) that makes some say it is shameful to grant favors to lovers, but those who say this are looking at the base lovers' untimeliness (akairia) and injustice (adikia), since nothing done properly (kosmios) and lawfully (nomimos) would justly bear blame."
textual_basis: "At 182a: the law about eros in other cities is easy to understand, for it is simply defined (haplos gar horistai); but here and in Sparta it is complicated (poikilos, 182b). At 182b: in Elis and Boeotia and where men are not clever at speaking (hou me sophoi legein), it is simply legislated as noble to grant favors to lovers; no one young or old would say it is shameful; the reason: so they don't have trouble trying to persuade the young by speech (logoi), since they are incapable of speaking (adynatoi legein, 182b). In Ionia and many other places, wherever people live under barbarians (hypo barbarois oikousin), it is considered shameful (182b). At 182a: the base lovers have produced the disgrace (oneidos) so that some dare to say it is shameful to grant favors to lovers; they say this looking at these men, seeing their untimeliness (akairia) and injustice (adikia), since surely nothing done properly (kosmios) and lawfully (nomimos) would justly bear blame."
limits: "This observation records the comparative taxonomy Pausanias deploys within this span. It does not assess whether his descriptions of Elis, Boeotia, or Ionia are historically accurate, or whether the taxonomy is internally consistent. It does not claim that this comparative move is meant to prepare a defense of Athenian paiderastia as superior to both the simplistic approval and the barbarian disapproval."
review_status: accepted
```

  - "Ἀριστογείτονος ἔρως"
  - "Ἁρμοδίου φιλία"
  - "κατέλυσεν αὐτῶν τὴν ἀρχήν"
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: erotic_argument
feature_id: feature_candidate_1210
feature_label: eros_as_civic_ordering_force
observation: "At 182b-182c, Pausanias argues that barbarian disapproval of pederasty is rooted in tyranny's structural need to suppress anything that produces proud thoughts and strong bonds among the ruled. He links three practices — eros, philosophy, and love of gymnastics — as the specific targets of barbarian/tyrannical prohibition. The explanation is functional: it does not benefit rulers for great thoughts (phronemata megala) or strong friendships and associations (philias ischyras kai koinonias) to arise among the ruled, and eros especially produces these (ho de malista philei ta te alla panta kai ho eros empoiein, 182c). He then provides a proof by deed (ergoi): the eros of Aristogeiton and the friendship of Harmodius, having become firm (bebaios), destroyed their rule (katelusen auton ten archen, 182c). The Athenian tyrannicide serves as a historical exemplum confirming that eros is genuinely subversive of tyranny."
textual_basis: "At 182b-182c: for barbarians, because of tyrannies, this (pederasty) is shameful, and so are philosophy and love of gymnastics (he te philosophia kai he philogymnastia). For I think it does not benefit the rulers for great thoughts (phronemata megala) to arise among the ruled, nor strong friendships and associations (philias ischyras kai koinonias) — which eros especially is disposed to produce, among all other things (ho de malista philei ta te alla panta kai ho eros empoiein, 182c). And the tyrants here learned this by deed (ergoi de touto emathon kai hoi enthade tyrannoi): for the eros of Aristogeiton and the friendship of Harmodius, having become firm (bebaios genomene), destroyed their rule (katelusen auton ten archen, 182c). The argument moves from structural explanation (tyranny suppresses what empowers subjects) to empirical confirmation (the Athenian tyrannicide is the proof)."
limits: "This observation records the argument linking eros, philosophy, and gymnasia as structural threats to tyranny, confirmed by the Harmodius-Aristogeiton exemplum. It does not assess whether Pausanias's historical claim about the Peisistratid tyranny is accurate, whether the link between philosophy and eros here anticipates or contrasts with other speeches in the Symposium, or whether the argument is meant to valorize Athenian democratic institutions by contrast with tyranny. It does not claim that Pausanias is making a coded political argument about contemporary Athens."
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "At 182d-183a, Pausanias catalogues extreme behaviors that lovers perform toward beloveds — supplications, swearing oaths, sleeping on doorsteps, and willing slavish servitude worse than any slave — and argues that these same behaviors, if performed for any other pursuit (money, office, power), would bring the greatest reproaches and be blocked by friends and enemies alike. The lover alone is praised for such conduct and the Athenian law grants him license. He then adds a striking exception clause: the only other pursuit for which such behavior would bring reproach rather than praise is — after a textual corruption — philosophy. The argument establishes that identical conduct is evaluated oppositely based solely on the purpose for which it is undertaken, and that eros holds a unique socially-licensed status that even philosophy does not share."
textual_basis: "At 182d-182e, the Athenian law gives the lover license to attempt to win the beloved and to be praised for performing wondrous deeds. At 183a, the counterfactual: if someone tried to do such things for money, office, or power — supplications, begging, swearing oaths, sleeping on doorsteps, willing to serve slavish servitudes such as no slave would endure — he would be impeded by both friends and enemies. The text then reads: 'except this, philosophy would reap the greatest reproaches.' The exception clause names philosophy as the one other pursuit that would receive reproach, structurally opposite to eros which is granted grace (see greek_terms) by the law."
limits: "This observation records the catalogue of lover behaviors, the purpose-based double-standard argument, and the philosophy exception. It does not assess whether the textual corruption affects the meaning, whether Pausanias is making a claim about philosophy's social status, or whether this anticipates the later speeches that connect eros and philosophy. It does not claim that the argument is meant ironically."
review_status: accepted
```

  - "πᾶσαν ἐξουσίαν"
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: erotic_argument
feature_id: feature_candidate_1212
feature_label: eros_licenses_exceptional_conduct
observation: "At 183b-183c, Pausanias identifies as the most striking thing the claim, attributed to the many, that the lover alone receives forgiveness from the gods for breaking oaths, because Aphrodisian oaths are said not to be oaths. This is presented as the culmination of the evidence that the Athenian custom treats loving as something altogether noble: both gods and humans have given the lover total license. The claim structurally extends the pattern of exceptional treatment for lovers from the social into the divine realm — not only are the lover's extreme behaviors socially praised, but he is uniquely exempt from divine sanction for perjury when the oath concerns eros."
textual_basis: "At 183b: the lover doing all these things is attended by grace, and it is granted by the law to act without reproach, as one accomplishing a most noble deed. At 183b-183c: 'The most striking thing, as the many say, is that for him alone who swears oaths there is forgiveness from the gods if he departs from the oaths — for they say that an Aphrodisian oath is not an oath.' Thus both gods and humans have made every license for the lover, as the law here says. The term rendered as forgiveness is the same used for pardon; the claim that certain oaths do not bind is attributed to popular belief rather than to Pausanias's own assertion, but it is deployed as the strongest piece of evidence in the first half of his two-sided argument."
limits: "This observation records the claim about divine forgiveness for erotic perjury and its structural role in the argument. It does not assess whether the popular belief is accurately reported, whether Pausanias endorses or merely cites it, or whether the idea of non-binding erotic oaths has a known source in Greek religion or folklore. It does not claim that this divine license has philosophical implications beyond its rhetorical function in Pausanias's speech."
review_status: accepted
```

feature_id: feature_candidate_1164
feature_label: criterion_for_evaluative_classification
observation: "In the span 182d-183d, Pausanias constructs a two-sided argument about the Athenian custom regarding pederasty. First (182d-183c), he presents the evidence that the custom treats loving as most noble: lovers are praised for extreme behaviors, forgiven for oath-breaking, and granted every license by gods and men. Then (183c-183d), he introduces the contrary evidence: fathers set pedagogues over beloveds to prevent them from conversing with lovers; age-mates and companions reproach any such conduct; and elders do not stop or rebuke those who reproach, implying endorsement of the reproach. He states that looking at this evidence, one would think pederasty is considered most shameful in Athens. He then resolves the apparent contradiction by restating the principle he introduced earlier in his speech: the matter is not simple, the thing itself is neither noble nor shameful, but done nobly it is noble and done shamefully it is shameful. The two-sides structure is an explicit argumentative device that enacts the complexity of the Athenian custom rather than merely asserting it."
textual_basis: "At 183c, the first conclusion: on this account one would think that both loving and becoming friends with lovers is considered most noble in this city. At 183c-183d: but whenever fathers set pedagogues over the beloveds and do not allow them to converse with lovers, and age-mates and companions reproach if they see such a thing happening, and the elders do not prevent those reproaching nor abuse them as speaking incorrectly — looking at these things one would think again that such a thing is considered most shameful here. At 183d: but the matter is not simple, just as was said at the beginning, that it is neither noble in itself nor shameful, but done nobly it is noble, done shamefully it is shameful. The verb 'was said from the beginning' explicitly marks the restatement as a return to Pausanias's earlier principle."
limits: "This observation records the two-sides argument structure and the explicit restatement of the principle. It does not assess whether the contrary evidence (pedagogues, age-mate reproach) accurately reflects Athenian practice, whether Pausanias's resolution is satisfactory, or whether the argumentative structure is meant to model sophistical antilogy. It does not claim that Pausanias's restatement resolves all tensions in his speech."
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "At 183e, Pausanias introduces a permanence criterion to distinguish the praiseworthy lover from the base one. The lover of the body is not constant (monimos) because he loves something not constant (oude monimou eron pragmatos). When the bloom of the body fades, he flies away (oichetai apoptamenos), shaming his many words and promises. By contrast, the lover of a good character (ethous chrestou ontos) remains through life (dia biou menei), fused with something constant (monimoi syntakeis). The criterion is structural: the permanence of the love object determines the permanence of the lover, and this in turn determines whether the lover is worthy."
textual_basis: "At 183e, Pausanias describes the body-lover: 'the one loving the body more than the soul; for he is not even constant (monimos), since he loves something not constant (oude monimou eron pragmatos). For at the same time as the bloom of the body (somati anthei) fades, which he loved, he flies away (oichetai apoptamenos), shaming many words and promises.' Then the character-lover: 'but the lover of a good character (ethous chrestou ontos) remains through life (dia biou menei), fused with something constant (monimoi syntakeis).' The law wants to test these men well (toutous de bouletai ho hemeteros nomos eu kai kalos basanizein). The contrast is drawn through the repeated monimos family of words, applied first to the lover, then to the love-object, then back to the lover who merges with the constant object."
limits: "This observation records the permanence criterion Pausanias introduces at 183e. It does not assess whether the body/soul contrast is philosophically coherent, whether the metaphor of the lover fusing with his object extends beyond Pausanias's speech, or whether this criterion is consistent with behavioral criteria Pausanias introduced earlier in his speech. It does not claim that this distinction anticipates the Diotima ladder."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_symposium_0032
source_work: Symposium

greek_terms:
  - βασανίζειν
  - ἀγωνοθετῶν
  - ἁλίσκεσθαι ταχὺ
  - χρόνος
  - χρημάτων
  - πολιτικῶν δυνάμεων
  - βέβαιον
  - γενναίαν φιλίαν
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_1164
feature_label: criterion_for_evaluative_classification
observation: "At 184a-184b, Pausanias describes the Athenian law as an umpire (agonotheton) that tests (basanizein) which camp the lover belongs to and which the beloved. The law proceeds by excluding three grounds for the beloved's capture as shameful: first, being caught quickly (to haliskesthai tachy), so that time may intervene, which is held to test most things well; second, being caught by money (chrematon); third, being caught by political powers (politikon dynameon), whether by cowering under suffering or by failing to despise benefits in money or political transactions. The rationale is that none of these things seems either stable (bebaion) or constant (monimon), apart from the fact that noble friendship (gennaian philian) does not grow from them. After these three exclusions, only one path remains under the Athenian law if the beloved is to grant favors to a lover nobly."
textual_basis: "At 184a, the law 'wants to test these men well and nobly (eu kai kalos basanizein), and to grant favors to some, and to flee from others.' The law encourages pursuit for some and flight for others, 'acting as umpire and testing (agonotheton kai basanizon) of which sort each is, the lover and the beloved.' At 184a-184b: 'for this reason first being caught quickly is considered shameful, so that time may intervene, which is held to test most things well (hos de dokei ta polla kalos basanizein); then being caught by money and by political powers is shameful, whether cowering when suffering and not enduring, or not despising when benefited in money or in political transactions.' At 184b: 'For none of these seems either stable (bebaion) or constant (monimon), apart from the fact that noble friendship (gennaian philian) does not grow from them. One path remains for our law (mia de leipetai toi hemeteroi nomoi hodos), if the beloved is to grant favors to a lover nobly.'"
limits: "This observation records the law's testing function and the three exclusionary criteria at 184a-184b. It does not assess whether Pausanias's description of Athenian custom is accurate, whether the exclusion of money and political power reflects democratic ideology, or whether the single remaining path he will propose is the only one his argument supports. It does not claim that the agonotheton metaphor prefigures the competitive structure of the symposium itself."
review_status: accepted
```

greek_terms:
  - δουλεία ἑκούσιος
  - ἐθελοδουλεία
  - ἀρετήν
  - συμβαλεῖν εἰς ταὐτόν
  - παιδεραστίαν
  - φιλοσοφίαν
  - φρόνησιν
  - παίδευσιν
  - μοναχοῦ ἐνταῦθα

english_gloss: ""
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_1164
feature_label: criterion_for_evaluative_classification
observation: "At 184c-184e, Pausanias identifies the single remaining path: two Athenian laws must converge (symbalein eis tauton) for it to be noble for a beloved to grant favors to a lover. The first law concerns pederasty: the lover's willing slavery of any kind to the beloved is not flattery or reproachful (184c). The second law concerns philosophy and the rest of virtue: if someone willingly serves another hoping to become better in wisdom or any part of virtue, this voluntary slavery is not shameful or flattery (184c). These two laws must be brought together — the one about pederasty and the one about philosophy and the rest of virtue (184c-184d). The convergence occurs when lover and beloved meet, each holding his law: the lover is justified in any service to the beloved who has granted favors, and the beloved is justified in any service to the one making him wise and good (184d). The lover must be able to contribute to wisdom and virtue, and the beloved must be in need of education and wisdom (184d-184e). When these laws converge, and only here (monachou entautha), it turns out noble for the beloved to grant favors to a lover — nowhere else (allothi de oudamou, 184e). Pausanias adds a final clause: in this case even being deceived is not shameful, whereas in all other cases being deceived brings shame (184e)."
textual_basis: "At 184c: 'just as for lovers, their willing slavery (douleuein ethelonta) in any slavery to the beloved is not flattery or reproachful, so too there remains one other voluntary slavery alone that is not reproachful; and this is the one concerning virtue (he peri ten areten).' At 184c: 'it is established among us that if someone is willing to serve another supposing that through him he will become better in wisdom or in any other part of virtue, this voluntary slavery (autē hē ethelodouleia) is not shameful nor flattery.' At 184c-184d: 'these two laws must be brought together (dei de to nomo touto symbalein eis tauton), the one about pederasty and the one about philosophy and the rest of virtue.' At 184d: 'when lover and beloved come together, each holding his law, the one justified in serving in any way the beloved who has granted favors, the other justified in serving in any way the one making him wise and good.' At 184d-184e: 'the one able to contribute to wisdom and the rest of virtue, the other needing to acquire education and the rest of wisdom.' At 184e: 'then when these laws come together into the same, only here (monachou entautha) does it turn out noble for the beloved to grant favors to a lover, nowhere else (allothi de oudamou). Even being deceived in this case is not shameful.'"
limits: "This observation records the two-law convergence structure at 184c-184e and its exclusivity claim. It does not assess whether Pausanias's argument successfully establishes that only the virtue-seeking case is noble, whether the asymmetry of the convergence (lover gives wisdom, beloved gives favors) is problematic, or whether the claim that deception is not shameful in this case undermines the testing rigor the law was supposed to provide. It does not claim that Pausanias's convergence anticipates the philosophical-eros synthesis in Diotima's speech."
review_status: accepted
```

The phrase ek tou parachrema marks the speech as extemporaneous and improvised rather than prepared. The verb symballomai frames the speech as a contribution to a shared enterprise, echoing the eranos terminology Eryximachus used when proposing the encomium competition."
textual_basis: "At 185c, immediately after concluding the argument that only the virtue-directed convergence of the two laws makes granting favors noble, Pausanias says: tauta soi, ephe, hos ek tou parachrema, o Phaidre, peri Erotos symballomai. The closing is addressed to Phaedrus, who was identified as the originator of the complaint that no one praises Eros. The verb symballomai (I contribute, I throw in my share) and the phrase ek tou parachrema (from the immediate moment, on the spur of the moment) frame the speech as an impromptu offering."
limits: "This observation records the closing formula and its vocabulary. It does not assess whether Pausanias's claim of extemporaneity is genuine or a rhetorical convention, whether the address to Phaedrus is significant beyond the dramatic frame, or whether this closing formula parallels or contrasts with closings of other speakers in the dialogue."

He immediately interrupts the narrative with a metanarrative aside: 'for the wise teach me to speak like this, balancing clauses' (didaskousi gar me isa legein houtosi hoi sophoi). The narrator self-consciously draws attention to his own stylistic technique — isocolon or balanced phrasing — and attributes it to instruction by unnamed sophoi. This is one of the few moments in the dialogue where the narrative frame itself becomes the object of explicit stylistic commentary."
textual_basis: "At 185c, the text reads: Pausaniou de pausamenou — didaskousi gar me isa legein houtosi hoi sophoi — ephe ho Aristodemos dein men Aristophane legein... The pun pairs the proper name Pausanias with the aorist participle pausamenou (from pauo, to stop or pause). The narrator breaks the fourth wall of the reported narrative to explain his stylistic choice, citing the sophoi as his teachers. The phrase isa legein refers to balanced or parallel phrasing, a feature of rhetorical prose associated with Gorgias and other sophists."
limits: "This observation records the wordplay and the metanarrative aside at 185c. It does not assess whether the aside is authentically Aristodemus's voice or a layer added by Apollodorus or Plato, whether the reference to the sophoi is ironic or sincere, or whether the stylistic self-consciousness has implications for the dialogue's treatment of rhetoric. It does not claim that the wordplay on Pausanias's name carries thematic weight."
review_status: accepted
```

feature_label: dramatic_disruption_or_pause
observation: "At 185c-185e, the planned sequence of speeches is disrupted by a bodily event: Aristophanes, who should speak after Pausanias, is seized by a hiccup (lynga) — attributed to overeating (plesmones) or some other cause — and cannot speak. Aristophanes addresses Eryximachus, who as a doctor (iatron) is reclining below him, and presents a binary demand: either cure the hiccup or speak in his place. Eryximachus agrees to do both and offers three escalating medical remedies: holding breath for a long time (apneusti), gargling with water (hydati anakogchyliason), and if the hiccup is very strong, tickling the nose to induce sneezing (ptare) once or twice. The hiccup is the specific bodily mechanism by which the speaking order is derailed, and a medical professional manages the disruption with escalating somatic interventions, swapping his turn with Aristophanes."
textual_basis: "At 185c-185d: after Pausanias pauses, Aristodemus reports that Aristophanes should speak next, 'but he happened to have been seized by a hiccup, either from overeating (hypo plesmones) or from some other cause, and was not able to speak.' Aristophanes addresses Eryximachus: 'Eryximachus, you are obliged either to stop my hiccup or to speak on my behalf, until I stop' (185d). Eryximachus replies: 'I shall do both: I will speak in your turn, and you, when you stop, in mine. While I am speaking, if the hiccup is willing to stop when you hold your breath (apneusti) for a long time — if not, gargle with water (hydati anakogchyliason). And if it is still very strong, take something with which you might tickle your nose and sneeze (ptare); and if you do this once or twice, even if it is very strong, it will stop' (185d-185e). The remedies escalate in intrusiveness: breath control, water gargling, sneeze induction. The seating arrangement (Eryximachus the doctor reclining below Aristophanes) is explicitly noted as enabling the request (185d)."
limits: "This observation records the hiccup as a bodily event that reorders the speaking turns and the medical remedies offered. It does not assess whether the hiccup is a comic device at Aristophanes's expense, whether the remedies reflect genuine Hippocratic practice or parody of medical discourse, or whether the disruption foreshadows Aristophanes's later speech content. It does not claim that the body's intrusion into the logoi carries philosophical significance about the relation between the somatic and the intellectual."
review_status: accepted
```

english_gloss: ""
feature_family: definition_ladder

feature_label: bifurcated_eros_extended_to_new_domain
observation: "At 186b-186c, Eryximachus opens his speech by extending Pausanias's two-Eros framework from the domain of human relationships to the domain of bodily physiology. He establishes that the nature of bodies possesses this double eros (ton diploun erota touton), because the healthy and the diseased are agreed to be different and unlike, and the unlike desires and loves the unlike. He then explicitly maps Pausanias's normative schema onto the body: just as Pausanias said it is noble to gratify good men and shameful to gratify the intemperate, so too in bodies themselves it is noble and necessary to gratify the good and healthy elements of each body — and this is what is called the medical art — while it is shameful and necessary to withhold gratification from the bad and diseased elements, if one is to be skilled (technikos). The prior speaker's bifurcation of Eros is thus re-ground in a different ontological domain (somatic rather than interpersonal) while retaining the same normative structure."
textual_basis: "At 186b: 'I shall begin speaking from the medical art, so that we may also honor the craft. For the nature of bodies possesses this double eros; for the healthy of the body and the diseased are agreed to be different and unlike, and the unlike desires and loves the unlike. So the eros for the healthy is one, and the eros for the diseased is another.' At 186b-186c: 'It is just as Pausanias was just now saying, that it is noble to gratify good men and shameful to gratify the intemperate — so too in bodies themselves it is noble and necessary to gratify the good and healthy elements of each body, and this is what is called the medical art, while to the bad and diseased it is shameful and one must withhold gratification, if one is to be skilled.' The verb tense 'was just now saying' (artios elegen) explicitly credits Pausanias. The mapping from human beloveds to bodily elements is marked by houto kai ('so too')."
limits: "This observation records the explicit transfer of the Pausanian framework to a new domain. It does not assess whether the mapping from interpersonal ethics to bodily physiology is valid, whether Eryximachus faithfully represents Pausanias's distinction, or whether the medical definition that follows is philosophically or medically sound."
review_status: accepted
```

  - "διαγιγνώσκων"
  - "μεταβάλλειν"
  - "ἐμποιῆσαι"
  - "ἐξελεῖν"
  - "δημιουργός"
english_gloss: ""

feature_id: feature_candidate_173
feature_label: craft_redefined_in_erotic_terms
observation: "At 186c-186d, Eryximachus offers a compact definition of medicine entirely in the vocabulary of eros: medicine is the knowledge of the body's erotics with respect to repletion and depletion (episteme ton tou somatos erotikon pros plesmonen kai kenosin). He then defines the most medical practitioner as the one who discriminates (diagnosis) the noble and shameful eros in these matters, and as the one who can effect transformation — changing one eros for another, implanting eros where it is absent, and removing it where it is present. The practitioner is called a good craftsman (agathos demiourgos). Four distinct erotic operations are attributed to medical skill: discrimination, transformation, implantation, and removal of eros. The craft of medicine is not merely described by analogy to eros; it is redefined as a form of erotic expertise."
textual_basis: "At 186c: 'For medicine, to speak in summary, is the knowledge of the body's erotics with respect to repletion and depletion.' At 186c-186d: 'And the one discriminating the noble and shameful eros in these matters — this man is the most medical. And the one who makes a change, so that instead of one eros they acquire the other, and in those where eros is not present but should come to be, knowing how to implant it, and being present to remove it — he would be a good craftsman (agathos demiourgos). For he must be able to make the things in the body that are most hostile become friendly and love each other.' The definition (estin gar iatrike ... episteme) presents the essential claim; the four operations (discrimination, transformation, implantation, removal) enumerate what erotic expertise in the bodily domain consists of."
limits: "This observation records Eryximachus's definition of medicine in erotic terms and the four operations he attributes to the medical practitioner. It does not assess whether this definition is coherent, whether it captures actual Greek medical practice, or whether the erotic vocabulary is meant literally or metaphorically. It does not claim that Eryximachus's speech subverts or transcends the craft analogy tradition in Plato."
review_status: accepted
```

  - "τὸ ἓν γάρ φησι διαφερόμενον αὐτὸ αὑτῷ συμφέρεσθαι"
  - "ἁρμονίαν τόξου τε καὶ λύρας"
  - "ἁρμονία"
  - "συμφωνία"
  - "ὁμολογία"
  - "ἀδύνατον"
english_gloss: ""

feature_id: feature_candidate_196
feature_label: predecessor_claim_contradicted
observation: "At 187a-187b, Eryximachus cites Heraclitus as someone who wishes to say (bouletai legein) the same thing he himself is arguing — that music works through Eros — but does not speak well in his words (tois ge rhemasin ou kalos legei). He quotes what appears to be a Heraclitean fragment: 'the one, disagreeing with itself, agrees with itself, like the harmony of bow and lyre.' Eryximachus then argues this is a great illogicality (polle alogia): harmony cannot be said to disagree or to exist from things still disagreeing. He offers a corrected formulation: harmony is a consonance (symphonia), which is an agreement (homologia), and agreement is impossible from things disagreeing, as long as they disagree. Rather, harmony comes from previously disagreeing elements (high and low) that subsequently come to agreement through the musical art. A pre-Socratic philosopher is named, quoted, criticized for his wording, and offered a logical revision — all within an encomiastic speech about Eros."
textual_basis: "At 187a: 'Music is also obvious to anyone who pays even a little attention, that it holds in the same way as these things — as perhaps Heraclitus also wishes to say, since in his words at least he does not speak well. For he says the one, disagreeing with itself, agrees with itself, like the harmony of bow and lyre. But it is a great illogicality to say that harmony disagrees or exists from things still disagreeing.' At 187a-187b: 'But perhaps he wished to say this: that from previously disagreeing elements, the high and the low, then later having come to agreement, harmony has come to be through the musical art. For surely harmony could not be from high and low still disagreeing; for harmony is consonance (symphonia), and consonance is a kind of agreement (homologia) — and agreement from things disagreeing, as long as they disagree, is impossible; and what disagrees and does not agree is impossible to harmonize.' The verb bouletai legein ('wishes to say') credits Heraclitus with correct intent but incorrect expression. The correction replaces the paradoxical identity-claim with a temporal sequence (from prior disagreement to subsequent agreement)."
limits: "This observation records the citation, quotation, and correction of Heraclitus within Eryximachus's speech. It does not assess whether Eryximachus's reading of Heraclitus is accurate, whether his logical revision improves on or distorts the original, or whether this moment of philosophical engagement has dramatic significance for Eryximachus's character. It does not claim that the Heraclitean fragment is authentically quoted or that Eryximachus's correction is meant to position him as a philosopher rather than a doctor."
review_status: accepted
```

He then adds a domain restriction: in the mere abstract composition (systasei) of harmony and rhythm, it is not difficult to discern erotic matters and the double Eros is not yet present there. The double Eros only emerges when harmony and rhythm must be used with respect to human beings — either in original composition (melopoieia) or in the correct use of pre-composed songs and meters (paideia). At that point, the matter becomes difficult and requires a good craftsman."
textual_basis: "At 187c: 'And music in turn is the knowledge of erotics concerning harmony and rhythm.' The restriction follows immediately: 'And in the mere composition of harmony and rhythm it is not difficult to discern the erotic matters, nor is the double Eros yet present there; but when it is necessary to use rhythm and harmony with humans — either composing, which they call melopoieia, or correctly using composed songs and meters, which has been called paideia — there indeed it is difficult and requires a good craftsman.' The phrase oude ho diplous eros entautha po estin (the double Eros is not yet present there) marks the abstract-structural level as prior to or exempt from the erotic bifurcation. The erotic definition of music here directly mirrors Eryximachus's earlier erotic definition of medicine."
limits: "This observation records the erotic definition of music and the domain-restriction claim within 187c. It does not assess whether the claim that abstract harmony lacks the double Eros is coherent, whether this restriction is consistent with Eryximachus's earlier treatment of medicine, or whether the melopoieia/paideia distinction maps onto a broader Platonic concern with the application of crafts to human beings. It does not claim that the restriction anticipates themes in later speeches."

observation: "At 187d-187e, Eryximachus maps the two Erotes onto two Muses, extending Pausanias's Ouranian/Pandemic framework onto a new divine register. The noble Eros — directed at orderly people and making the not-yet-orderly more orderly — is identified as the Eros of the Ouranian Muse. The pandemic Eros is identified as belonging to Polyhymnia. The Polyhymnia Eros must be applied with caution: one should reap its pleasure without implanting licentiousness. Eryximachus illustrates with an analogy from his own craft: just as in medicine it is a great task to use desires concerning the cookery art well, so as to reap pleasure without sickness."
textual_basis: "At 187d-187e: 'One should gratify and guard the eros of orderly people, and so that those not yet orderly may become more orderly — and this is the noble, the heavenly Eros, the Eros of the Ouranian Muse. The Polyhymnia one is the pandemic, which one must apply with caution to those to whom one applies it, so as to reap its pleasure but implant no licentiousness, just as in our craft it is a great task to use well the desires concerning the cookery art, so as to reap pleasure without sickness.' The Ourania/Polyhymnia pair replaces the Ourania/Pandemos Aphrodite pair from Pausanias's speech. The cookery analogy (opsopoiiken technen) provides a medical parallel for the cautious management of pandemic Eros."
limits: "This observation records the Muse assignment and the cookery analogy at 187d-187e. It does not assess whether the Muse pairing is traditional or invented, why Polyhymnia in particular is chosen for pandemic Eros, or whether the cookery analogy carries evaluative weight. It does not claim that Eryximachus's framework is superior to or derivative of Pausanias's."
review_status: accepted
