observation: "At 187e-188b, Eryximachus universalizes the double-Eros framework beyond crafts to the cosmic and religious domains. He first states the universal scope at 187e: both Erotes must be guarded in music, medicine, and all other things both human and divine (anthropoeiois kai tois theiois), as far as possible, because both are present. He then projects the framework onto the seasonal cycle: the constitution of the yearly seasons is full of both Erotes. When the orderly Eros governs the hot, cold, dry, and wet, producing temperate harmony and blending, it brings good harvest (eueterian) and health to humans, animals, and plants — and does no injustice. When the hubristic Eros becomes dominant in the seasons, it destroys and injures. Plagues, dissimilar diseases in beasts and plants, frosts, hail, and blights arise from the pleonexia and disorder (akosmias) of such erotics toward each other. He then names the relevant science: astronomy is the knowledge of these things concerning star movements and yearly seasons. Finally, at 188b, he extends the framework to religion: all sacrifices and everything over which mantic art presides concern the guarding and curing of Eros."
textual_basis: "At 187e: 'And in music and in medicine and in all other things both human and divine, as far as possible, each Eros must be guarded; for both are present (eneston).' At 187e-188a: 'Since even the constitution of the yearly seasons is full of both these, and when the hot and cold and dry and wet happen upon the orderly Eros toward each other — the things I was just now speaking of — and receive temperate harmony and blending, they come bringing good harvest and health to humans and other animals and plants, and do no injustice. But when the Eros with hubris becomes more dominant concerning the yearly seasons, it destroys many things and does injustice.' At 188a-188b: 'For plagues are accustomed to arise from such things and many other dissimilar diseases both for beasts and plants; for frosts and hail and blights arise from the pleonexia and disorder of such erotics toward each other, the science of which concerning star movements and yearly seasons is called astronomy.' At 188b: 'And further, all sacrifices and the things over which mantic art presides — these are the matters concerning gods and humans concerned with guarding and curing Eros.' The term eneston (both are present) at 187e asserts the ontological universality of the double Eros, grounding the cosmic and religious extensions."
limits: "This observation records the cosmic and religious extensions and the universal claim within 187e-188b. The segment cuts off mid-phrase at 188b; the remaining content of Eryximachus's speech concerning mantic art is not covered by this span. This observation does not assess whether Eryximachus's seasonal theory reflects genuine Presocratic or medical cosmology, whether the definition of astronomy in erotic terms is original or conventional, or whether the mantic/sacrificial extension is logically coherent with the earlier craft redefinitions. It does not claim that Eryximachus is constructing a systematic natural theology or that this universalization is meant as authoritative rather than rhetorical amplification."
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "At 188c-188d, Eryximachus extends his erotic redefinition of crafts to mantic art. He states that all human community (koinonia) concerns the guarding and curing of Eros (188c). All impiety (asebeia) arises when one gratifies the other Eros rather than the orderly (kosmios) Eros in matters concerning parents, the dead, and the gods (188c). The mantic art is assigned the task of overseeing and curing lovers in these matters (188c). It is then defined as the craftsman (demiourgos) of friendship between gods and humans, through its knowledge of human erotics as they tend toward divine law (themis) and piety (eusebeia, 188d). Mantic art is defined not merely as erotic knowledge but specifically as a mediating craft that produces friendship across the divine-human boundary."
textual_basis: "At 188c: 'the community of humans with each other — it concerns nothing other than the guarding and curing of Eros. For all impiety is accustomed to arise if one does not gratify the orderly Eros nor honor him and hold him first in every deed, but the other one, concerning parents both living and dead and concerning gods.' At 188c-188d: 'These things have been assigned to the mantic art to oversee and to cure lovers, and in turn the mantic art is the craftsman of friendship between gods and humans through knowing the human erotics, as many as tend toward divine law and piety.' The term demiourgos is the same word Eryximachus has used for craftsman throughout his speech. The definition follows the erotic-redefinition pattern established earlier within Eryximachus's speech, but adds the divine-human mediating claim."
limits: "This observation records the erotic definition of mantic art and its distinctive divine-human mediating function within Eryximachus's speech at 188c-188d. It does not assess whether this definition reflects actual Greek religious practice, whether Eryximachus's sequence of craft redefinitions is philosophically coherent, or whether the mantic art's mediating role anticipates or contrasts with later speeches. It does not claim that Eryximachus is an authoritative spokesman for Platonic views on religion."

  - ἀναπληρῶσαι

english_gloss: ""
feature_family: turn_geometry
feature_id: feature_candidate_720
feature_label: explicit_procedure_control
observation: "At 188d-188e, Eryximachus closes his encomium with three distinct moves: a summation distinguishing the whole Eros from the particular Eros oriented toward goods with moderation and justice, which he says has the greatest power and provides all happiness (188d); a self-deprecating incompleteness claim ('perhaps I too, in praising Eros, leave out many things, though not willingly,' 188e); and a formal handoff to Aristophanes ('if I have left anything out, it is your task, Aristophanes, to fill it in,' 188e). The handoff offers Aristophanes two options: fill in what Eryximachus omitted, or praise the god in another way if he has something else in mind. The closing references the hiccup that had caused Aristophanes to swap turns with Eryximachus earlier ('since you have stopped your hiccup,' 188e)."
textual_basis: "At 188d: 'Thus the whole of Eros has much and great, or rather all power taken together, but the one concerning good things, completed with moderation and justice both among us and among gods — this one has the greatest power and provides all happiness to us and makes us able to associate with each other and be friends and with the gods who are stronger than us.' At 188e: 'Perhaps then I too, in praising Eros, leave out many things, though not willingly. But if I have left anything out, it is your task, Aristophanes, to fill it in; or if you have some other way in mind to praise the god, praise him, since you have stopped your hiccup.' The verb paraleipo means to leave aside or omit; exelipon is from ekleipo, to leave out; anaplerosai is to fill up or complete. The reference to the hiccup ties the closing back to the bodily disruption earlier in the dialogue."
limits: "This observation records the closing formula and handoff at 188d-188e. It does not assess whether Eryximachus's incompleteness claim is sincere or conventional, whether the handoff to Aristophanes has dramatic significance for the content of Aristophanes's speech, or whether addressing the hiccup in the closing is a comic touch or a natural dramatic transition. It does not claim that this handoff structure recurs across other speeches in the Symposium."
review_status: accepted
```

  - πταρμὸν

english_gloss: ""
feature_family: turn_geometry
feature_id: feature_candidate_720
feature_label: explicit_procedure_control
observation: "At 189a-189b, before Aristophanes delivers his encomium, a meta-theatrical exchange negotiates the conditions under which comic speech is permitted at the symposium. Aristophanes opens by marveling that the orderly part of the body (to kosmion tou somatos) desires noises and tickles like the sneeze — a remark that Eryximachus immediately characterizes as joke-making (gelotopoieis) when about to speak. Eryximachus appoints himself guardian (phylaka) of Aristophanes's speech, threatening to intervene if Aristophanes says anything laughable (geloion), though Aristophanes could speak in peace (189a-189b). Aristophanes responds with a distinction: he does not fear saying laughable things (geloia) — that would be gain and native to his Muse (tes hemeteras mouses epichorion) — but he fears saying things that are ridiculous (katagelasta, 189b). Eryximachus retorts that Aristophanes will not escape by throwing a blow and demands he speak as one who will give an account (hos doson logon, 189b). The exchange establishes a frame in which comic speech is permitted only under the threat of a guardian's intervention and the requirement of accountability."
textual_basis: "At 189a, Aristophanes, picking up from Eryximachus's handoff, says the hiccup stopped only when he applied the sneeze to it, 'so that I wonder if the orderly part of the body desires such noises and tickles as the sneeze is; for it stopped immediately when I applied the sneeze to it.' Eryximachus replies: 'Good Aristophanes, watch what you are doing. You are making jokes when about to speak, and you force me to become a guard of your speech, in case you say something laughable, when you could speak in peace' (189a-189b). Aristophanes laughs and says: 'You speak well, Eryximachus, and let what I have said be unsaid. But do not guard me, since I am afraid about what will be said — not that I might say laughable things — for that would be gain and native to our Muse — but that I might say ridiculous things' (189b). Eryximachus: 'Having thrown a blow, Aristophanes, do you think you will escape? Pay attention and speak as one who will give an account' (189b)."
limits: "This observation records the negotiation about comic speech, the geloia/katagelasta distinction, and the guardian-of-speech role Eryximachus claims at 189a-189b. It does not assess whether Aristophanes's subsequent myth honors or violates these negotiated conditions, whether the geloia/katagelasta distinction is philosophically significant, or whether Eryximachus's demand for accountability foreshadows Socratic dialectical norms. It does not claim that the exchange is a Plato-authored apology for Aristophanes's comic vocation."
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "At 189d-189e, Aristophanes opens his myth with a claim about a lost human nature. He states that originally there were three human genera (tria ta gene), not two as now — male (arren) and female (thely). The third genus, the androgynon, was common to both and has now vanished, its name remaining only as a term of reproach (en oneidei onoma keimenon). The taxonomy is explicitly presented as a past state contrasted with the present, where only two genders remain and the third exists only as a pejorative label."
textual_basis: "At 189d, Aristophanes declares one must first learn human nature and its affections (ten anthropinen physin kai ta pathemata autes), because our ancient nature was not the same as now but different. He then enumerates: 'First, there were three genera of humans, not two as now, male and female, but a third was also present, common (koinon on) to both of these, whose name is now left but which itself has vanished. For androgynon was then one kind, both form and name, common from both male and female, but now it does not exist except as a name lying in reproach' (189d-189e). The temporal frame is explicit: tote men ... nyn de; the third gender has disappeared as a reality and survives only as an insult."
limits: "This observation records the tripartite taxonomy and the claim that the third gender has vanished except as a pejorative label. It does not assess whether Aristophanes's myth reflects actual Greek gender discourse, whether the androgynon corresponds to any historical or cultural category, or whether the 'name in reproach' claim has ironic or comic force. It does not claim that this taxonomy maps onto later philosophical treatments of gender in Plato."
review_status: accepted
```

The all-male was originally the offspring (ekgonon) of the sun; the all-female was offspring of the earth; the androgynous genus, which partook of both, was offspring of the moon — and the reason given is that the moon partakes of both (sun and earth). This genealogy then explains the primeval humans' spherical shape: they were spherical (periphere) and their locomotion was spherical because they were similar to their cosmic parents."
textual_basis: "At 190a-190b, the text explicitly links the three genera to three cosmic bodies: 'There were three genera and of such a kind because the male was originally the offspring of the sun (heliou ekgonon), the female of the earth (ges), and the one partaking of both of the moon (selenes), because the moon partakes of both. And they were spherical and their motion was spherical because of being similar to their parents (tois goneusin homoia einai).' The reasoning is structured as: cosmic parentage explains the existence of three genera, and physical similarity to those parents explains the spherical shape and rotational locomotion already described (the eight-limbed cartwheeling at 190a)."
limits: "This observation records the cosmic genealogy and its explanatory function within Aristophanes's myth. It does not assess whether the sun-earth-moon genealogy draws on known mythological traditions, whether the logic of similarity is meant to be taken literally or as comic invention, or whether this cosmic taxonomy has philosophical implications beyond the myth's dramatic context."
review_status: accepted

english_gloss: ""

feature_id: feature_candidate_1213
feature_label: mythic_divine_punishment_mechanism
observation: "At 190b-190c, the primeval humans' hybristic assault on the gods creates a specific dilemma for Zeus and the other gods. The dilemma is structured by two unacceptable alternatives: they cannot kill the humans with thunderbolts as they did the giants, because this would destroy the honors (timai) and sacrifices (hiera) that humans provide to the gods; nor can they allow the humans to continue their wanton violence (aselgainein). The gods are in aporia (eporoun). The constraint is economic-theological: the gods depend on human cult for their honors, so annihilation is self-defeating. Zeus eventually devises a mechanism (mechanen) that will both preserve humans and stop their licentiousness (akolasia) by making them weaker."
textual_basis: "At 190b, the primeval humans are described as terrible in strength and vigor, having great thoughts (phronemata megala), and attacking the gods — and what Homer says about Ephialtes and Otus is said about them, the attempt to make an ascent to heaven to attack the gods. At 190c: 'Zeus and the other gods deliberated what they should do with them and were at a loss (eporoun). For they could not kill them and, as with the giants, blast the race with thunderbolts and make it vanish — for their honors (timai) and sacrifices (hiera) from humans would vanish — nor could they let them behave wantonly. With difficulty Zeus, having thought, said: I think I have a mechanism (mechanen), so that humans may both exist and cease their licentiousness (akolasia) by becoming weaker.' The aporia is explicitly stated; the two horns of the dilemma are clearly distinguished; the resolution is introduced by mogis ('with difficulty')."
limits: "This observation records the dilemma structure and the cult-economic constraint at 190b-190c. It does not assess whether the idea that gods depend on human honors is traditional Greek theology or Aristophanic comic invention, whether the Ephialtes-Otus citation is accurate to Homer, or whether the dilemma's resolution (splitting humans in two) is philosophically significant. It does not claim that the dependence of gods on human cult is a Platonic theological position."
review_status: accepted
```

The punishment sentence is not a single decree but a conditional structure: the present bisection is to both weaken them and make them more useful by increasing their number; the threat of a second bisection, reducing them to hopping on one leg, is held in reserve as a further deterrent."
textual_basis: "At 190d, Zeus declares: 'Now I shall cut each of them in two (diatemō dicha), and at once they will be weaker and at once more useful to us because they have become more in number; and they will walk upright on two legs. But if they still seem to behave wantonly and are unwilling to keep quiet, I shall again cut them in two (palin au ... temō dicha), so that they will go about on one leg, hopping (askōliazontes).' The conditional ean d' eti ... palin au marks the escalation clause as a threatened further step. The verb askōliazontes evokes the askōliasmos game of hopping on one leg at the Rural Dionysia — a comic image that undercuts the threat's menace with ridicule (190d-190e)."
limits: "This observation records the conditional structure of Zeus's punishment and the threatened second bisection. It does not assess whether the askōliasmos reference carries ritual or festival significance beyond its comic force, or whether the escalation clause is meant to be taken as a genuine threat within the myth or as part of the speech's comic register. It does not claim that this structure parallels any Platonic argument about divine punishment."

feature_label: mythic_aetiology
observation: "At 190e-191a, Apollo performs a series of surgical procedures on the bisected humans that function as an aetiology of human anatomy, explicitly connecting present bodily features to a mythic past. Zeus commands Apollo to turn each human's face and half-neck toward the cut so that, seeing his own bisection, the human may be more orderly (kosmiōteros). Apollo then draws the skin from all sides toward the belly, like a drawstring purse (ta systasta ballantia), makes one opening and ties it at the middle of the belly — this, the text states, is what they now call the navel (ton omphalon). He smoothes most wrinkles using an instrument like a cobbler's leather-smoothing tool around a last (kalapoda), but leaves a few around the belly and navel specifically as a memorial (mnēmeion) of the ancient suffering (tou palaiou pathous). The navel is thus textually marked as a surviving trace of a past event, and the bodily wrinkles around it as deliberately preserved reminders."
textual_basis: "At 190e, Zeus cuts the humans in two 'like those who cut eggs (oa) for preserving, or like those who cut eggs with hairs.' He commands Apollo: 'whomever he cut, he ordered Apollo to turn the face and the half of the neck toward the cut, so that seeing his own cutting the human might be more orderly, and he ordered him to heal the rest.' At 190e-191a: 'And he turned the face, and drawing the skin from all sides toward what is now called the belly, like drawstring purses (hōsper ta systasta ballantia), making one opening he tied it at the middle of the belly — which indeed they call the navel. And the many other wrinkles he smoothed out and articulated the chest, having some such tool as the cobblers have for smoothing the wrinkles of leather around the last (kalapoda). But he left a few, those around the belly itself and the navel, to be a memorial (mnēmeion) of the ancient suffering.' The explicit naming of the navel (ho dē ton omphalon kalousi) and the memorial language (mnēmeion einai) mark this as a self-conscious aetiology."
limits: "This observation records the aetiological structure of the surgery passage and the navel's designation as a memorial. It does not assess whether Aristophanes's aetiology is original or draws on existing mythic traditions about the navel, whether the craftsman similes (egg-cutters, drawstring purse, cobbler's last) have philosophical or literary resonance beyond vividness, or whether the kosmiōteros rationale for the face-turning carries thematic weight for the dialogue's concern with order and shame. It does not claim that the mnēmeion language anticipates Platonic theories of recollection."
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "At 191b-191c, after the first divine solution (bisection) fails — the halves die from starvation and inactivity because they do nothing but embrace, unwilling to do anything apart from each other — Zeus takes pity (eleēsas) and provides a second mechanism (allēn mēchanēn): he relocates the genitals to the front. The passage includes an explicit contrast with the prior state: previously the genitals were external and reproduction occurred by emission into the earth, like cicadas (hōsper hoi tettiges, 191c). The new arrangement produces internal reproduction (genesis en allēlois, male into female) and serves two distinct purposes: (a) in male-female union, offspring are born and the species continues; (b) in male-male union, satiety (plēsmonē) results from the intercourse, so that they may desist (diapauointo), turn to work, and attend to the rest of life. The second mechanism thus differs from the first in kind: the first addressed hubris by weakening and multiplying; the second addresses the unintended fatal consequence of the first by enabling discharge and return to productive life."
textual_basis: "At 191b: after the bisection, each half desiring its own half came together, throwing arms around each other and intertwining, desiring to grow together (symphynai), and they died from hunger and other inactivity (argias) because they were unwilling to do anything apart from each other. And whenever one half died and the other was left, the remaining one sought another and intertwined with it... and in this way they were perishing. At 191b-191c: 'Taking pity (eleēsas) on them, Zeus provides another mechanism (allēn mēchanēn poreizetai), and relocates their genitals (ta aidoia) to the front — for previously they also had these outside, and they begot and gave birth not into each other but into the earth, like cicadas (hōsper hoi tettiges) — and he relocated them thus to the front and through these made generation in each other (tēn genesin en allēlois epoiēsen), through the male in the female, for the sake of this: so that in the intertwining, on the one hand if a man should meet a woman, they would beget and the race would come to be, and on the other hand if also male should meet male, satiety (plēsmonē) at least would come to be from the intercourse and they would desist (diapauointo) and turn to work and attend to the rest of life.' The pity motive and the marking of a second mechanism contrast with the punitive rationale of the first bisection."
limits: "This observation records the second divine mechanism, the contrast between prior external and new internal reproduction, and the dual-purpose structure. It does not assess whether the cicada comparison (hōsper hoi tettiges) carries specific literary or philosophical resonance — for instance, the cicada myth in the Phaedrus — or whether the relegation of male-male union to satiety rather than generation has normative implications. It does not claim that the two mēchanai structure maps onto a philosophical argument about eros and procreation, or that Aristophanes's narrative prefigures Diotima's ladder of erotic purposes. The segment cuts off mid-phrase at 191c with esti de oun ek tosou; the observation covers only what is within the cited span."
review_status: accepted
```

greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_1164
feature_label: criterion_for_evaluative_classification
observation: "At 185a-185b, Pausanias caps his definition of noble eros by addressing the case of deception. He presents two parallel cases: being deceived about a lover's wealth is shameful because it reveals the beloved was acting for money; but being deceived about a lover's virtue is noble because it reveals the beloved was acting for virtue and self-improvement. From this he concludes that any gratification given for the sake of virtue is entirely noble, and identifies this eros as the Ouranian Eros. The exception clause for deception-about-virtue is the final move that completes the definition."
textual_basis: "At 185a, Pausanias says if one gratifies a lover as rich for money's sake but the lover turns out poor, the deception is shameful because it shows the beloved would serve anyone for money. At 185a-185b, he contrasts: if one gratifies a lover as good and expecting to become better through the friendship, but the lover turns out bad and lacking virtue, the deception is still noble because it reveals the beloved was acting for virtue and self-improvement. At 185b he concludes that anything done for virtue and self-improvement is noblest, and that this is the eros of the heavenly goddess."
limits: "This observation records the final argumentative move in Pausanias's speech and its structural role in capping the definition. It does not assess whether the deception exemption is logically consistent with the earlier testing-and-time criteria Pausanias discussed, nor whether the conclusion that all virtue-seeking gratification is noble is meant as his complete position. It does not claim that this exemption clause anticipates or contradicts later speeches."
review_status: accepted
```

greek_terms:
  - "διπλοῦν εἶναι τὸν ἔρωτα"
  - "τέλος ἐπιθεῖναι τῷ λόγῳ"
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: turn_geometry
feature_id: feature_candidate_1122
feature_label: argument_transition_marker
observation: "At 185e-186a, Eryximachus takes the floor by declaring that Pausanias began well but did not complete his argument adequately, so Eryximachus must try to put an end (telos epitheinai toi logoi) to the speech. He praises Pausanias's distinction of a double Eros (diploun einai ton erota) as well made, but announces that Eros is not limited to human souls toward beautiful men — it exists in animal bodies, plants, and all existing things. He grounds this universal extension in his medical art. The opening performs a double move: validating the predecessor's conceptual framework while declaring it incomplete, and announcing a scope expansion grounded in the speaker's own techne."
textual_basis: "At 185e-186a, Eryximachus says: 'It seems necessary to me, since Pausanias, having set out upon the speech well, did not complete it adequately, for me to try to put an end to the speech. For the double Eros seems to me well distinguished; but that it is not only in the souls of humans toward beautiful men but also toward many other things and in other things — in the bodies of all animals and in what grows from the earth and, so to speak, in all existing things — I think I have observed from medicine, our craft.' The verb telos epitheinai (to put an end) and the scope expansion are the two key moves marking the transition."
limits: "This observation records Eryximachus's opening structure and the scope-expansion claim. It does not assess whether Eryximachus's criticism of Pausanias as insufficiently complete is fair, whether his medical grounding justifies the universalization, or whether the scope expansion is consistent with the rest of his speech. It does not claim that this is the correct or definitive account of Eros in the dialogue as a whole."
review_status: accepted
```

feature_label: mythic_origin_taxonomy
observation: At 191d-192a, Aristophanes maps contemporary erotic orientation types to the three original genera from the myth. Men from the androgynous genus become woman-lovers (philogynekes) and include most adulterers. Women from the androgynous genus become man-loving (philandroi) and adulteresses. Women from the all-female genus are turned toward women, and from this genus come female companions (hetairistriai). Men from the all-male genus pursue males, are naturally most courageous, and when grown become political men.
textual_basis: At 191d-191e: 'As many men as are a cut of the common genus (which was then called androgynon), they are woman-lovers, and most adulterers come from this genus. As many women as are a cut of the common genus, they are man-loving and adulteresses. As many women as are a cut of woman, these are not at all attentive to men but are turned rather toward women, and hetairistriai come from this genus.' At 191e-192a: 'As many as are a cut of the male pursue males... they are best of boys and youths, being naturally most courageous. Some say they are shameless, lying; for they do this not from shamelessness but from boldness, courage, and manliness, embracing what is like themselves. A great proof: when fully grown, these alone turn out to be political men.'
limits: This observation records the mapping of erotic orientation types to the three mythic genera at 191d-192a. It does not assess whether this taxonomy is meant to be taken literally, whether it captures or distorts actual Athenian sexual categories, or whether the categorization of adulterers and hetairistriai carries normative weight beyond the descriptive schema.
review_status: accepted
```

Those who spend their whole lives together cannot say what they want from each other — sexual intercourse is clearly not the purpose of their meeting. The soul of each wants something else (allo ti) which it cannot express but divines and hints at.
textual_basis: At 192b-192c: 'When someone meets his own actual half, then they are struck wonderfully by friendship, familiarity, and love, unwilling to be separated from each other even for a short time. Those who spend their lives together through life are these, who could not even say what they want from each other. For it would not seem to anyone that this is the sexual intercourse, that for this reason one rejoices in being with the other with such great seriousness; but the soul of each obviously wants something else which it cannot express but divines and hints at.'
limits: This observation records the phenomenological description of erotic union and the claim that the soul's desire is inexpressible at 192b-192c. It does not assess whether this ineffability claim anticipates the ladder of love in Diotima's speech, whether the rejection of sexual pleasure as the primary purpose is consistent with the genitals-relocation narrative in Aristophanes's own speech, or whether the allo ti has a specific philosophical referent.

feature_label: mythic_definition_of_eros
observation: At 192d-192e, Aristophanes introduces a hypothetical scenario within his mythic speech: Hephaestus appears to a pair of lovers embracing, carrying his tools, and asks what they want from each other. When they are at a loss, he asks if they desire to be fused together into one, so that being two they become one and live and die together as a single being. The passage reports that no one would refuse this offer, and each would think they had heard what they had long desired: to be fused and melted together with the beloved, becoming one from two.
textual_basis: At 192d-192e: 'And if Hephaestus, standing over them as they lie together, holding his tools, should ask: "What is it you want, humans, to have from each other?" And if they were at a loss, he should again ask: "Is this what you desire, to be in the same place as much as possible with each other, so as not to be apart night or day? For if you desire this, I wish to melt you together and fuse you into the same, so that being two you become one, and while you live, as one, both live in common, and when you die, there in Hades instead of two you are one, dead in common." Hearing this, we know that no one would deny it, nor would appear to want anything else, but simply would think they had heard what they had long desired, coming together and being melted with the beloved, from two becoming one.' The hypothetical is marked by the optative construction (eroito) and the particle ei (if), establishing it as a thought-experiment within the mythic frame.
limits: This observation records the Hephaestus thought-experiment as a rhetorical device within Aristophanes's myth. It does not assess whether the fusion scenario implies a literal physical merging, whether the role of Hephaestus as craftsman-god is significant beyond his function as a convenient divine agent of fusion, or whether this thought-experiment anticipates later philosophical treatments of unity in the Symposium.
review_status: accepted
```

  - ἡγεμὼν
  - στρατηγός
  - εὐσεβεῖν
  - εὔδαιμον
  - ἀρχαίαν φύσιν
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: closure_type
feature_id: feature_candidate_192
feature_label: myth_close
observation: At 193b-193c, Aristophanes closes his mythic speech with a hortatory passage: everyone must urge piety toward the gods so that we may escape evils and achieve good, with Eros as leader and commander. He then addresses Eryximachus directly, preemptively denying that his speech refers to Pausanias and Agathon in particular — though he concedes they may be among the male-natured. He makes a universal claim: human happiness will come if we complete eros and each finds his own beloved, returning to the original nature. Since this is the best, the nearest thing among present options is best: finding a beloved naturally suited to oneself.
textual_basis: At 193b: 'For this reason every man must urge everything to be pious toward the gods, so that we may escape the former and achieve the latter, with Eros as our leader and commander. Let no one act contrary to him — and whoever is hateful to the gods acts contrary to him. For becoming friends and reconciled with the god, we will find and meet our own beloveds, which few of the present company do.' At 193b-193c: 'And let Eryximachus not take me up, comically mocking my speech, as if I mean Pausanias and Agathon — for perhaps they do happen to be among these and are both male in nature — but I speak concerning all, both men and women, that thus our race would become happy if we complete eros and each obtains his own beloved, returning to the original nature. And if this is best, necessarily among present circumstances the nearest thing to this is best; and this is to obtain a beloved naturally suited to oneself.' The closing shifts from mythic narrative to direct hortatory address and a defensive qualification.
limits: This observation records the closing structure of Aristophanes's speech at 193b-193c. It does not assess whether the preemptive defense against Eryximachus is motivated by actual comic mockery, whether the 'original nature' claim is consistent with the earlier myth, or whether the concluding pragmatism modifies the utopian claim. It does not claim that this closing prefigures the content of later speeches.
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "At 194d, Phaedrus interrupts Socrates's questioning of Agathon to assert procedural authority over the encomium order. He warns that if Agathon answers Socrates, Socrates will lose all concern for the proceedings, needing only someone to converse with, especially a beautiful one. Phaedrus states his own duty is to oversee the encomium to Eros and accept a speech from each person, and insists that after each has completed his speech to the god, then they may converse."
textual_basis: "At 194d, Phaedrus is reported to have interrupted (hypolabonta) to say: 'Agathon, if you answer Socrates, he will care no more at all about anything here, provided he has someone to converse with, especially a beautiful one. But I enjoy hearing Socrates converse, yet it is necessary for me to oversee the encomium to Eros and to receive a speech from each one of you. Having each paid his due to the god, then let him converse.' The verb hypolambanein indicates interrupting or taking up the thread; the imperative frames Phaedrus's assertion of procedural authority."
limits: "This observation records the interruption and enforcement of speech order at 194d. It does not assess whether Phaedrus's claimed authority is recognized by other participants or whether the interruption is motivated by concern for the encomium order or by a desire to protect Agathon from Socratic elenchus."
review_status: accepted
```

english_gloss: ""

feature_id: feature_candidate_071
feature_label: assent_chain
observation: "At 194a-194d, Socrates leads Agathon through a series of elicited agreements. Socrates first recalls Agathon's courage before the theater audience when he went on stage with actors (194b). He then elicits Agathon's agreement that to a man with sense, a few thoughtful people are more terrifying than many foolish ones (194b). Socrates pursues the implication: Agathon would be more concerned about those he considers wise, would feel shame before them if doing something shameful, but would not feel shame before the many (194c). Agathon assents to each step. Phaedrus interrupts at 194d before any refutation or contradiction is drawn."
textual_basis: "At 194a, Agathon accuses Socrates of trying to drug him (pharmattein) so he will be flustered (thorybēthō). Socrates responds at 194b by recalling Agathon's courage on the stage (epi ton okribanta) before the theater. At 194b-194c, the assent chain proceeds: Agathon agrees that to a man with sense (noun ephonti) a few thoughtful men (oligoi emphrones) are more terrifying than many foolish ones (pollōn aphronōn phoberōteroi). Socrates asks if Agathon would be more concerned about those he considers wise, feeling shame (aishynoio) before them if doing something shameful, but not before the many (194c). Agathon responds alethē legeis to the first part. Phaedrus interrupts at 194d before Socrates obtains a conclusion from the final question."
limits: "This observation records the assent chain structure and its interruption at 194d. It does not assess whether the elenchus was leading toward a refutation or aporia, whether Agathon's agreement indicates genuine conviction or polite deference, or whether the interruption has dramatic significance beyond procedural enforcement."
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "At 193e, Eryximachus responds to Aristophanes's request not to mock his speech by naming Socrates and Agathon as 'deinoi' concerning erotic matters. As it is, he is confident. The remark characterizes the remaining two speakers as having recognized expertise in the domain the encomia address."
textual_basis: "At 193e, Eryximachus says: 'I will obey you, and my speech was spoken pleasantly. And were I not aware that Socrates and Agathon are terribly clever (deinois) concerning erotic matters (peri ta erōtika), I would be very afraid that they would be at a loss for words (aporēsōsi logōn) because so many and varied things have been said. But as it is, I am confident (tharrō).' The domain is specified as peri ta erōtika; the alternative (being at a loss for words) and the confidence frame the labeling as marking the two remaining speakers as specially qualified."
limits: "This observation records Eryximachus's characterization of Socrates and Agathon at 193e. It does not assess whether this characterization is accurate, ironic, or self-serving. It does not claim that Eryximachus's confidence is justified or that his praise is sincere. It does not interpret the significance of labeling both Socrates and Agathon with the same term despite their different approaches to erotics."

  - οἷος οἵων αἴτιος
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: turn_geometry
feature_id: feature_candidate_720
feature_label: explicit_procedure_control
observation: At 194e-195a, before beginning his encomium, Agathon announces that all previous speakers have made a methodological error: they did not praise the god Eros himself (ou ton theon enkōmiazein) but instead congratulated humans on the goods Eros causes (tous anthrōpous eudaimonizein tōn agathōn hōn ho theos autois aitios). Agathon declares the correct method (heis de tropos orthos pantos epainou) is first to describe what sort the subject is in himself (oios estin), then to describe his gifts (tas doseis). He then states he will follow this program.
textual_basis: At 194e, Agathon responds to Phaedrus's interruption and then announces his approach: first I wish to say how I must speak, then to speak. He criticizes all previous speakers (pantes hoi prosthen eirēkotes) for failing to describe the god himself. At 195a he states the single correct method for every praise: to go through in speech what sort the subject is and of what things he is the cause. He then applies this to Eros: thus it is just for us to praise Eros, first what he is like, then his gifts.
limits: This observation records Agathon's methodological program as announced within the text. It does not assess whether Agathon actually follows his own method, whether his criticism of previous speakers is accurate or fair, or whether the method is philosophically sound.
review_status: accepted
```

english_gloss: ""
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_196
feature_label: predecessor_claim_contradicted
observation: At 195a-195c, Agathon asserts that Eros is the youngest of the gods (neōtatos theōn, 195a) and always young (aei neon, 195c). He explicitly marks disagreement with a claim that Eros is older than Cronus and Iapetus: I agree about many other things, but this I do not agree with (touto ouch homologō), that Eros is older than Cronus and Iapetus (195b-195c). He provides supporting argument: the violent old deeds among gods (castrations, bonds) happened through Necessity (Anankēi), not through Eros, because if Eros had been present there would have been friendship and peace (philia kai eirēnē) instead. Eros is said to rule the gods now (ex hou Erōs tōn theōn basileuei, 195c).
textual_basis: At 195a, Agathon first claims Eros is youngest: first of all, youngest of the gods. At 195b-195c he explicitly marks disagreement: I, though agreeing about many other things, this I do not agree with, that Eros is older than Cronus and Iapetus, but I say he is the youngest of the gods and always young. The old deeds about gods, which Hesiod and Parmenides speak of, happened through Necessity and not through Eros, if those spoke truly; for there would have been no castrations nor bonds nor other violent acts if Eros had been among them, but friendship and peace, just as now, since Eros rules the gods.
limits: This observation records Agathon's claim that Eros is youngest and his argument against the claim that Eros is oldest. It does not assess whether Agathon's argument is valid, whether his interpretation of Hesiod and Parmenides is accurate, or whether the disagreement between speakers has dramatic significance.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_symposium_0067
source_work: Symposium
stephanus_span: 195d-195e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/symposium.txt

greek_terms:
  - Ἄτην
  - ἁπαλοί πόδες
  - κατ᾽ ἀνδρῶν κράατα βαίνει
  - ἁπαλός
  - ἤθεσι καὶ ψυχαῖς
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: myth_demarcation
feature_id: feature_candidate_427
feature_label: poetic_citation_as_argument
observation: At 195d-195e, Agathon uses a passage from Homer about the goddess Atê as analogical evidence that Eros is soft (hapalos). He states that a poet like Homer is needed to demonstrate a god's softness (195d), then cites Homer's description of Atê: her feet are soft, she walks not on the ground but on men's heads (195d). Agathon approves this evidence: it is a good proof of softness that she does not walk on hard but on soft. He then applies the same reasoning to Eros (tōi autōi dē kai hēmeis chrēsometha tekmēriōi peri Erōta hoti hapalos, 195e): Eros does not walk on earth or skulls but walks and dwells in the softest of existing things — in characters and souls (ēthesi kai psuchais) of gods and humans. Eros avoids hard characters and settles in soft ones. Since Eros always touches the softest with his feet, he is necessarily the softest.
textual_basis: At 195d: there is need of a poet such as Homer to show a god's softness. Homer said Atê is a goddess and soft — her feet soft — quoting: her feet are soft, for she does not walk on the ground, but goes upon men's heads. Agathon says this is a good proof (kalōi tekmēriōi) of softness because she does not walk on hard but on soft. At 195e: we will use the same evidence about Eros that he is soft. For he does not walk on ground or on skulls, which are not very soft, but in the softest of beings he both walks and dwells. He has settled in characters and souls of gods and humans, and avoids those with a hard character and settles in soft ones.
limits: This observation records the use of a Homeric passage about Atê as analogical evidence for Eros's softness. It does not assess whether the Homeric citation is used accurately, whether the analogical inference is valid, whether Agathon's use of Homer differs in kind from earlier speakers' use of poetic authority, or whether the characterization of Eros as soft is meant seriously or ironically.
review_status: accepted
```

greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_1101
feature_label: characteristic_catalogue_for_definiendum
observation: "Agathon attributes the four canonical virtues to Eros in a structured sequence: justice (Eros neither does nor suffers injustice, uses no violence, all service is voluntary, 196b-196c); temperance (Eros rules over pleasures and desires, which is the agreed definition of temperance, 196c); courage (Eros masters Ares the most courageous, 196c-196d); and wisdom (Eros is a poet who makes others poets, and governs the arts, 196d-196e). Each virtue receives a distinct argument."
textual_basis: "At 196b-196e, after concluding the treatment of beauty, Agathon states he must speak about Eros's virtue. Justice: 'Eros neither does injustice nor suffers injustice... violence does not touch Eros... everyone willingly serves Eros, and what is voluntarily agreed upon, the kings of the city, the laws, say is just' (196b-196c). Temperance: 'to temperance Eros has a very great share; for temperance is agreed to be ruling over pleasures and desires; but no pleasure is stronger than Eros... Eros ruling over pleasures and desires would be outstandingly temperate' (196c). Courage: 'not even Ares withstands Eros; for Ares does not have Eros, but Eros has Ares... the one having is stronger than the one had; so mastering the most courageous of all, he would be most courageous of all' (196c-196d). Wisdom: 'the god is a wise poet so as to make another a poet; everyone becomes a poet whom Eros touches... what one does not have or know, one could neither give to another nor teach' (196d-196e)."
limits: "This observation records the structure and content of the four-virtue catalogue applied to Eros at 196b-196e. It does not assess whether the arguments for each virtue are valid, whether the four virtues map to a canonical Platonic or popular list, or whether the sequence is meant to be exhaustive. It does not claim that Agathon's virtue catalogue is original or derivative."
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: "At 198a, Aristodemus reports that all present applauded, judging the speech appropriate to both the speaker and the god. At 198b, he continues: 'How, blessed one, am I not going to be at a loss, both I and anyone else, who is about to speak after such a beautiful and varied speech? The rest was not similarly amazing, but at the end, the beauty of words and phrases — who would not be astounded hearing it?' He adds that from shame he almost ran away."
limits: "This observation records the audience applause, Socrates's claim that his fear was prophetic, his stated aporia, and his praise of Agathon's rhetorical style. It does not assess whether Socrates's aporia is genuine or ironic, whether the audience's approval carries dramatic weight, or whether his self-characterization as near-fleeing from shame is a sincere emotional response or a rhetorical posture."

textual_basis: "At 198d, Socrates says he thought due to foolishness that one must tell the truth about the subject being praised, select the finest from the truths, and arrange them as becomingly as possible. At 198e, he says the correct method is to attribute the greatest and most beautiful things to the subject, whether they hold thus or not; falsehood is no matter. At 199a, he states the aim is to make Eros appear the most beautiful and best, clearly to those who do not know."
limits: "This observation records Socrates's description of the two methods of praise. The stated goal is to elicit agreement from him before speaking himself. Phaedrus grants permission at 199c. This marks a procedural shift from epideictic monologue to Socratic question-and-answer, and Socrates immediately begins interrogating Agathon."
textual_basis: "At 199b, Socrates says to Phaedrus: Allow me to ask Agathon a few small things (pares moi Agathōna smikr' atta eresthai), so that having secured agreement from him (hin' anomologēsamenos par' autou) I may then speak. At 199c, Phaedrus gives permission: I yield; ask. The narrator reports: After this, Socrates began from somewhere around here. The verb anomologēsamenos marks the intent to establish premises through dialectical exchange before speaking."
limits: "This observation records the procedural request and its granting. It does not assess whether the questioning that follows is a genuine elenchus, a sophistical trap, or a rhetorical performance. It does not claim that this deferral changes the genre of Socrates's contribution from speech to dialectic in a formally binding way."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_symposium_0076
source_work: Symposium
stephanus_span: 199d-200c

  end_char: 58409
  text_sha256: "445ca4dae14f0dd6599e0281e312b88126ad08176864b5c7b491a107911f2e20"
greek_terms:
  - τινός
  - ἐπιθυμεῖ
  - οὐκ ἔχων
  - ἐνδεής
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_071
feature_label: assent_chain
observation: "At 199d-200c, Socrates leads Agathon through a sustained assent chain. First, at 199d-199e, Socrates establishes that Eros is love of something, using analogies to kinship relations (father-of-someone, mother-of-someone, brother-of-someone) to force the relational definition. Agathon agrees at each step. Then at 200a, Socrates elicits agreement that Eros desires what it is love of. At 200a-200b, Socrates asks whether the desirer has or lacks its object, then presses that what desires desires what it is lacking. Finally at 200b-200c, Socrates establishes that someone who already possesses a quality (big, strong, healthy) cannot desire that same quality. The chain implicitly refutes Agathon's encomium, which had attributed beauty, goodness, and other perfections to Eros."
textual_basis: "At 199d, Socrates asks whether Eros is the sort that is love of something or of nothing, analogizing through 'father is father of someone' and 'brother is brother of someone' (199d-199e). At 200a, he asks whether Eros desires what it loves. At 200a-200b, he asks whether the desirer has or lacks its object, then presses: what desires desires what it is lacking. At 200b-200c, he argues that someone big cannot desire to be big, someone strong cannot desire to be strong. Agathon assents to each step."
limits: "This observation records the structure of the assent chain and the sequence of agreed premises at 199d-200c. It does not assess whether each inference is logically valid, whether Agathon's agreement is sincere or coerced, or whether the chain successfully refutes Agathon's earlier encomium. It does not claim that the chain is complete on its own terms."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_symposium_0077
source_work: Symposium
stephanus_span: 200b-200c

  text_sha256: "76b07d78c5fb76ae5cb750b1f59f60ddd295e26c17977e720193e0850e6a079a"
greek_terms:
  - βούλοιτ' ἄν
  - ἐξαπατηθῶμεν
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_1083
feature_label: refutation_by_counterexample
observation: "At 200b-200c, Socrates explicitly addresses and dismantles a potential counterexample to the desire-of-lacking principle. Someone might think that a healthy person desires health, a strong person desires strength, a fast person desires speed — that is, that those who already possess a quality can also desire it. At 200b-200c, Socrates introduces a caution against being deceived, arguing that one necessarily has what one possesses at present, whether one wishes or not, and that no one would desire what they already have."
textual_basis: "At 200b-200c, Socrates says that if someone strong wishes to be strong, someone fast to be fast, someone healthy to be healthy — someone might think that those who are such and have these things also desire them — so that they may not be deceived, Socrates says this: these people necessarily have each of these things in the present, whether they wish or not, and no one would desire what they already have. The caution against deception is explicitly marked in the text, and the argument rejects the possibility of desiring what one already possesses."
limits: "This observation records the explicit deception-caution and the dismantling of the counterexample at 200b-200c. It does not assess whether the counterexample is genuinely deceptive, whether Socrates's argument fully resolves the puzzle it addresses, or whether the argument anticipates later Platonic treatments of desire and deficiency. It does not claim that this is a distinct refutation separate from the broader elenchus."
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: At 176c, after consolidating procedural control by polling the participants and declaring the strongest drinkers have given up, Eryximachus states that since no one present is eager to drink much wine, perhaps he will be less annoying by speaking the truth about what drunkenness is like. He adds that this has become clear to him from medicine, that drunkenness is difficult for humans (176c-176d). He then states the personal and advisory consequences: neither would he himself willingly drink far, nor would he advise another, especially someone still hungover from the previous day. The medical art is the stated epistemic ground for the drinking norm.
limits: This observation records the explicit appeal to medical authority as grounds for a procedural recommendation at 176c-176d. It does not assess whether the medical claim is correct, whether the appeal is sincere or rhetorical, or whether it is consistent with Eryximachus's later encomium.

textual_basis: "At 197a, Agathon asks: tēn tōn technōn dēmiourgian ouk ismen? The three adjectives mark the binary: ellogimos (distinguished/renowned) and phanos (bright/clear) for the touched, skoteinos (dark/obscure) for the untouched. The verb ephapsētai (touches) echoes the claim at the start of 196e that Eros touches (hapsētai) the poet."
limits: "This observation records the binary classification and its light-dark metaphor at 196e-197a. It does not assess whether the classification is exhaustive or theoretically coherent, whether the metaphor carries epistemological weight, or whether this binary is consistent with other speakers' views on craft expertise."

```yaml
observation_id: obs_symposium_0088
source_work: Symposium
stephanus_span: 200d-200e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/symposium.txt
  stephanus_span: 200d-200e
  start_marker: 200d
  end_marker: 200e
  start_char: 58409
  end_char: 59301
  text_sha256: 0dc0a156f94a718f06c7dfe388548f1496ff21e9362202aef1e1d9f00e87fa32
greek_terms:
  - βούλομαι
  - εἰς τὸν ἔπειτα χρόνον
  - παρεῖναι
  - σῳζόμενα
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_071
feature_label: assent_chain
observation: "Socrates leads Agathon through a temporal analysis of desire. When someone claims to desire present goods (wealth, health, strength), Socrates shows that the actual meaning is a wish that these present goods continue to be present in future time. The desire is thus revealed to be for what is not yet secured. Agathon agrees to each step."
textual_basis: "At 200d, Socrates presents the case: someone possessing wealth, health, and strength says they wish to continue having these in future time. Socrates analyzes: 'when you say you desire present things, do you mean anything other than this, that you wish the present things to be present also in future time?' The text reports Agathon agreed (symphanai ephē ton Agathōna). Socrates then concludes: 'Is this then loving that thing, which is not yet ready for him nor does he have, that in future time these things be preserved and present to him?' The agreement at the start of 200e (panu ge) confirms the step."
limits: "This observation records the temporal-bridge step within the assent chain at 200d-200e. It does not assess whether the inference from desiring present things to desiring future possession is logically valid, whether Agathon's agreement is coerced or genuine, or whether this move suffices to refute Agathon's earlier encomium. It does not claim that this argument anticipates Diotima's treatment of eros as desire for permanent possession of the good."
review_status: accepted
```

feature_label: assent_chain
observation: "At 201a-201b, Socrates leads Agathon through a chain of agreements that refutes Agathon's earlier encomium claim that Eros is beautiful. Socrates first gets Agathon to recall his own claim that Eros is love of beauty, not ugliness (201a). He then secures the premise that one loves what one lacks (201b). From these he concludes that Eros lacks beauty, and that what lacks beauty is not beautiful. This directly contradicts Agathon's encomium which had attributed beauty to Eros as his defining quality."
textual_basis: "At 201a, Socrates asks Agathon to recall his claim that the gods' affairs were arranged through love of beautiful things, and Eros is love of beauty, not ugliness. Agathon confirms. At 201b, Socrates asks: 'Is it agreed that one loves what one lacks and does not have?' Agathon says yes. 'Then Eros lacks and does not possess beauty.' Agathon agrees: 'Necessarily.' 'Do you say that what lacks beauty and possesses no beauty at all is beautiful?' Agathon: 'No, by Zeus.' 'Then do you still agree that Eros is beautiful, if these things hold?'"
limits: "This observation records the structure and content of the assent chain at 201a-201b. It does not assess whether each premise is independently valid, whether Agathon's agreements are coerced or sincere, or whether the refutation is complete as an argument against all of Agathon's encomium claims about Eros. It does not claim that this refutation establishes a new positive definition of Eros."
review_status: accepted

The verb expressing tentative realization ('I am likely to,' 'I suspect') is paired with the infinitive phrase for 'to know nothing.' The introduction marks the transition from the Socratic refutation of Agathon to the reported narrative that will constitute Socrates's encomium."
textual_basis: "At 201d, Socrates says he will let Agathon go and instead go through the speech about Eros which he once heard from a Mantinean woman, Diotima. He characterizes her as wise in these things and many others, notes that she procured a ten-year delay of the plague for the Athenians when they were sacrificing before the disease, and states that she taught him erotics. observation: "At 201e, after Socrates infers that if Eros is not beautiful and not good, then Eros must be ugly (aischros) and bad (kakos), Diotima rejects the inference. She tells Socrates not to speak ill (ouk euphēmēseis) and asks whether he thinks that whatever is not beautiful must necessarily (anankaion) be ugly. She does not provide the positive middle term at this point but blocks the forced-choice inference by questioning whether the two alternatives are exhaustive."
textual_basis: "At 201e, the text reads: 'kai egō, pōs legeis, ephēn, ō Diotima; aischros ara ho Erōs esti kai kakos; kai hē, ouk euphēmēseis, ephē; ē oiei, hoti an mē kalon ē, anankaion auto einai aischron;' Socrates proposes the forced alternative (ara); Diotima rejects it with a rhetorical question introduced by ē oiei (or do you think). The word anankaion (necessarily, forced) is the inferential link she blocks. The phrase ouk euphēmēseis (don't speak ill, don't blaspheme) marks her rebuke as normative."
limits: "This observation records the rejection of the false dichotomy at 201e. It does not assess whether the inference from not-beautiful to ugly is actually fallacious, whether Diotima's rebuke is genuine or rhetorical, or whether the rejection of the forced alternative establishes a general principle about intermediate states."
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "At 202a, Diotima introduces and defines correct opinion (orthē doxa) as a middle state (metaxy) between wisdom (sophia/phronēsis) and ignorance (amathia). She provides a two-sided criterion: correct opinion is not knowledge (epistēmē) because it is an irrational thing (alogos) and knowledge must be rational; it is not ignorance because it hits the truth (tou ontos tynchanon). The definition establishes a formal middle term between two poles."
textual_basis: "At 202a, the text reads: 'to ortha doxazein kai aneu tou echein logon dounai ouk oisth', ephē, hoti oute epistasthai estin — alogon gar pragma pōs an eiē epistēmē; — oute amathia — to gar tou ontos tynchanon pōs an eiē amathia; — esti de dēpou toiouton hē orthē doxa, metaxy phronēseōs kai amathias.' The term metaxy (between) marks the intermediate status. The two arguments are: (1) it cannot be knowledge because it is alogon (without account), (2) it cannot be ignorance because it hits the truth. Socrates responds: alēthē, ēn d' egō, legeis (you speak truly)."
limits: "This observation records the definition of orthē doxa as an intermediate term at 202a. It does not assess whether this definition of knowledge is consistent with other Platonic dialogues, whether the orthē doxa concept is meant to parallel Eros's intermediate nature, or whether the definition is philosophically sound."
review_status: accepted
```

observation: When Socrates claims Eros is agreed by all to be a great god, Diotima laughs and asks: by all who do not know, or by those who know? When Socrates insists 'by all,' Diotima responds that Eros cannot be agreed to be a great god among those who say he is not even a god — listing herself and Socrates as those who hold this position. The laughter marks the claim as naive and the conventional consensus as unreliable.
textual_basis: At 202b-202c, Socrates states Eros is agreed by all to be a great god. Diotima asks: 'by all who do not know, or by those who know?' Socrates says: 'by all.' Diotima laughs (gelasasa) and asks: 'How could he be agreed to be a great god among those who say he is not even a god?' When Socrates asks who, she responds: 'One is you, one is me.' The laughter is explicitly recorded (gelasasa) and immediately precedes the challenge to the consensus claim.
limits: This observation records the laughter and the explicit self-inclusion as textual events. It does not assess whether Diotima is being ironic or sincere, whether Socrates is actually naive, or whether the claim that Eros is not a god is meant as a serious theological position.
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "At 202e, Diotima describes the function of the daimonic: it interprets and ferries (hermēneuon kai diaporthmeuon) between gods and humans — carrying prayers and sacrifices from humans to gods, and commands and recompense for sacrifices from gods to humans. Being in the middle (en mesōi) of both, it fills and completes (symplēroi) the gap so that the whole (to pan) is bound together (syndedesthai) with itself. The description establishes a systematic mediational structure between divine and mortal realms."
textual_basis: "At 202e, after naming Eros a great daimōn, Diotima describes what power (dynamis) the daimonic has: 'hermēneuon kai diaporthmeuon theois ta par' anthrōpōn kai anthrōpois ta para theōn, tōn men tas deēseis kai thysias, tōn de tas epitaxeis te kai amoibas tōn thysiōn, en mesōi de on amphoterōn symplēroi, hōste to pan auto hautōi syndedesthai.' Two directional verbs (hermēneuon, diaporthmeuon) specify the mediational activity; two pairs of content-bundles (prayers/sacrifices, commands/recompense) specify what is carried; the phrase en mesōi ōn amphoterōn repeats the intermediate status; symplēroi and syndedesthai specify the cosmic binding function."
limits: "This observation records the functional description of the daimōn as presented at 202e. It does not assess whether this description is original to Diotima or borrowed from existing Greek religious traditions, whether the binding-of-the-whole (to pan syndedesthai) is a cosmological or merely theological claim, or whether the description of daimonic function grounds specific claims about divination, priestly craft, and initiation rites mentioned in the continuation."
review_status: accepted
```

  text_sha256: c5d62ae189ae25eec68b9580fec970f905c73dee903983a782012df1df3d7df5

english_gloss: ""

feature_label: daimonic_mediation_function
observation: "At 203a, Diotima states that god does not mix with humans directly (theos de anthropoi ou meignytai). All intercourse and speech between gods and humans — both waking and sleeping — occurs through the daimonic. Eros is identified as one of these many and various daimones."
textual_basis: "At 203a, the text reads: 'theos de anthropoi ou meignytai, alla dia toutou pasa estin he homilia kai he dialektos theois pros anthropous, kai egeregosi kai katheudousi.' The claim of non-mixing (ou meignytai) and the directional mediation (dia toutou) specify that the daimonic is the exclusive medium of divine-human interaction. The passage concludes: 'toutoi de hoi daimones polloi kai pantodapoi eisin, heis de touton esti kai ho Eros.' The identification of Eros as one daimōn among many specifies his place within the daimonic category."
limits: "This observation records the explicit claim that gods do not mix directly with humans and that the daimonic is the exclusive medium of divine-human interaction. It does not assess whether this is consistent with Greek religious practice, whether it makes Eros a lesser being than a god, or whether the waking/sleeping distinction carries epistemological significance. It does not claim that Diotima's theology is Plato's own."
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "At 203a, Diotima draws a binary classification of expertise: one who is wise about daimonic matters (peri ta toiauta sophos) is called a 'daimonic man' (daimonios aner), while one who is wise about something else — either crafts (technas) or manual skills (cheirourgias tinas) — is called 'banausic' (banausos). The classification divides human wisdom into two exclusive categories: daimonic wisdom and technical/manual wisdom."
textual_basis: "At 203a, the text reads: 'kai ho men peri ta toiauta sophos daimonios aner, ho de allo ti sophos on e peri technas e cheirourgias tinas banausos.' The men ... de construction presents a binary contrast with two distinct labels. The first category is defined by domain (ta toiauta, referring to daimonic matters from the preceding sentence); the second by a disjunction of either crafts or manual skills. The label banausos is explicitly applied to the second category."
limits: "This observation records the binary classification of expertise presented at 203a. It does not assess whether this classification is exhaustive, whether the daimonic man label carries evaluative weight relative to the banausic label, or whether this distinction has philosophical implications for the dialogue's treatment of knowledge. It does not claim that the taxonomy is original to Diotima or that it anticipates later Platonic divisions of knowledge."
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "At 203b, after Socrates asks about Eros's parentage, Diotima marks the transition to a mythic narrative with a length disclaimer: 'it is longer to narrate; nevertheless I will tell you.' She then begins the Poros/Penia conception myth, set at the feast of the gods at Aphrodite's birth. The narrative transition is explicitly announced by the length disclaimer and the switch from dialectical exchange to continuous mythic storytelling."
textual_basis: "At 203b, Diotima responds to Socrates's question about Eros's father and mother: 'makroteron men, ephe, diegesasthai; homos de soi ero.' The comparative makroteron (longer) marks the anticipated narrative as extended; diegesasthai (to narrate/relate) signals the genre as narrative rather than argument. The myth begins with the temporal formula 'ote gar egeneto he Aphrodite' (when Aphrodite was born), setting the scene at the gods' feast. Poros, son of Metis, is introduced as drunken on nectar and sleeping in Zeus's garden; Penia arrives as a beggar."
limits: "This observation records the narrative transition marker and the myth's opening parameters at 203b. It does not assess whether the myth is meant to be taken literally, whether the Poros/Penia genealogy is original or traditional, or whether the myth's content resolves the definitional questions raised earlier in the speech. It does not claim that the length disclaimer has interpretive significance beyond its stated function."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms: []

feature_id: feature_candidate_1209
feature_label: mythic_origin_taxonomy
observation: "Within Diotima's mythic account of Eros as son of Poros and Penia, Eros's character traits are systematically divided between maternal (Penia/Need) and paternal (Poros/Resource) inheritance at 203c-203d. From Penia: always poor, hard, dry, barefoot, homeless, sleeping on the ground, always dwelling with need. From Poros: plotting against the beautiful and good, courageous, impetuous, intense, a terrible hunter, always weaving devices, desire of wisdom and resourceful, philosophizing throughout life, a terrible sorcerer, druggist, and sophist."
textual_basis: "At 203c, the text names Eros as son of Poros and Penia (Porou kai Penias hyos). At 203c-203d, Penia-derived traits are listed: pentes aei esti, skleros, auchmeros, anypodētos, aoikos, chamaipētēs, tēn tēs mētros physin echōn, aei endeia synoikos. At 203d, Poros-derived traits are listed: epiboulos esti tois kalois, andreios, itēs, syntonos, thēreutēs deinos, aei tinas plekōn mēchanas, phronēseōs epithymētēs kai porimos, philosophōn dia pantos tou biou, deinos goēs kai pharmakeus kai sophistēs."
limits: "This observation records the dual-inheritance structure as a feature of the mythic account at 203c-203d. It does not assess whether the traits are consistent with one another, whether the sorcerer/sophist characterization is positive or negative, or whether this portrait resolves or deepens the aporia about Eros's nature from the preceding elenchus. It does not interpret the philosophical significance of philosophōn dia pantos tou biou."
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "At 203e, Diotima concludes the mythic portrait by placing Eros in an intermediate epistemological position: in the middle between wisdom and ignorance (sophias te au kai amathias en mesō estin). The claim follows the claim that Eros is neither permanently resourceless nor permanently wealthy, and the intermediate epistemic position is presented as another dimension of Eros's between-status."
textual_basis: "At 203e, the text reads: 'hōste oute aporei Herōs pote oute ploutei, sophias te au kai amathias en mesō estin.' The phrase en mesō (in the middle) with the genitives sophias and amathias places Eros between the two epistemic poles. The structural placement of this claim as following from the alternation between living and dying and the constant outflow of resources establishes the epistemological intermediate as a consequence of the dual inheritance from Poros (resourceful) and Penia (needy)."
limits: "This observation records the intermediate epistemological claim about Eros at 203e within the span. It does not assess whether this claim is consistent with the earlier ortha doxa definition outside this span, whether it implies that Eros has correct opinion, or whether the intermediate epistemic status is meant to characterize the philosopher as distinct from the wise and the ignorant. It does not connect this claim to the metaxy framework established outside the observed span."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_symposium_0108
source_work: Symposium
stephanus_span: 204a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/symposium.txt
  stephanus_span: 204a
  start_marker: 204a
  end_marker: 204a
  start_char: 65708
  end_char: 66156
  text_sha256: 244ae245a1a1d1c4d8c8d798665318fd5c4544a1c47b043da47ec9b15c64319f
greek_terms:
  - θεῶν οὐδεὶς φιλοσοφεῖ
  - οὐ φιλοσοφεῖ
  - οὐδ' αὖ οἱ ἀμαθεῖς φιλοσοφοῦσιν
  - δοκεῖν αὑτῷ εἶναι ἱκανόν
  - μὴ οἰόμενος ἐνδεὴς εἶναι
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_222
feature_label: intermediate_term_defined
observation: "At 204a, Diotima establishes who philosophizes by excluding three classes: gods do not philosophize because they are already wise; the wise do not philosophize because they already possess wisdom; the ignorant do not philosophize because they think themselves sufficient despite lacking goodness, beauty, and intelligence. The inference is that one who does not feel a need does not desire what they think they do not need. This leaves an unstated intermediate class — those aware of their own deficiency — as the only possible philosophers."
textual_basis: "Three exclusions are stated explicitly at 204a: (1) no god philosophizes or desires to become wise because they already are wise (theōn oudeis philosophoi oude' epithymei sophos genesthai — esti gar); (2) nor does anyone else who is wise philosophize (ou philosophoi); (3) the ignorant do not philosophize nor desire to become wise (ou philosophoiousin oude' epithymousi). The reason given for the third exclusion: the very difficulty of ignorance is that while being neither beautiful and good nor intelligent, one thinks oneself sufficient (to dokoun hautōi einai hikanon). The general principle: the one who does not think himself in need does not desire what he does not think he needs (oukoun epithymei ho mē oiomenos endēs einai hou an mē oiētai epideisthai). Socrates then asks the question that forces the inference: who philosophizes if neither the wise nor the ignorant?"
limits: "This observation records the triple exclusion structure and the self-sufficiency diagnosis of ignorance. It does not assess whether the categories are exhaustive, whether the claim about ignorance as self-sufficiency is the only or best account of ignorance, or whether the inference to an intermediate class is valid. It does not claim that this is a complete definition of philosophy or that it represents Plato's considered view."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_symposium_0109
source_work: Symposium
stephanus_span: 204a-204b

greek_terms:
  - μεταξὺ τούτων ἀμφοτέρων
  - ἀναγκαῖον ἔρωτα φιλόσοφον εἶναι
  - μεταξὺ εἶναι σοφοῦ καὶ ἀμαθοῦς
  - πατρὸς μὲν γὰρ σοφοῦ
  - μητρὸς δὲ οὐ σοφῆς
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_222
feature_label: intermediate_term_defined
observation: "At 204b, Diotima completes the argument from the triple exclusion by stating that it is obvious even to a child that philosophers are those between (metaxy) the wise and the ignorant, and that Eros belongs to this class. The argument: Eros is love of the beautiful, wisdom is among the most beautiful things, therefore Eros is necessarily a philosopher (lover of wisdom), and being a philosopher he is between the wise and the ignorant. The cause (aitia) of this intermediate nature is Eros's dual parentage: a wise and resourceful father and a not-wise and resourceless mother."
textual_basis: "At 204b, Diotima says: 'It is clear even to a child that those between both — to which Eros also belongs' (dēlon dē ... hoti hoi metaxy toutōn amphoterōn, hōn an eiē kai ho Erōs). The argument follows: 'For wisdom is among the most beautiful things, and Eros is love concerning the beautiful, so necessarily Eros is a philosopher, and being a philosopher he is between the wise and the ignorant' (estin gar dē tōn kallistōn hē sophia, Erōs d' estin erōs peri to kalon, hōste anankaion erōta philosophon einai, philosophon de onta metaxy einai sophou kai amathous). The aitia statement: 'father wise and resourceful, mother not wise and resourceless' (patros men gar sophou esti kai euporou, mētros de ou sophēs kai aporou). The passage closes: 'This then is the nature of this daimon, dear Socrates.'"
limits: "This observation records the argument that Eros is necessarily a philosopher and the intermediate positioning. It does not assess whether the inference from 'Eros loves beauty' and 'wisdom is beautiful' to 'Eros loves wisdom' is a valid syllogism, whether the intermediate category is philosophically coherent, or whether the birth-causation explanation is meant as literal genealogy or mythic metaphor. It does not claim that this is Diotima's final or complete definition of Eros."
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "After Socrates cannot answer what belongs to the one who obtains beautiful things, Diotima changes the framing: she substitutes good for beautiful and asks the same questions about good things. When asked what the lover of good things loves, Socrates answers 'to become his own.' When asked what comes to the one who obtains good things, Socrates answers 'he will be happy.' The substitution of good for beautiful enables the definitional progression that had stalled."
textual_basis: "At 204d-204e, the text reports: all' ephē hōsper an ei tis metabalōn anti tou kalou tōi agathōi chrōmenos pynthanoito — phere, ō Sōkrates, erāi ho erōn tōn agathōn; ti erāi; genesthai, ēn d' egō, autōi. kai ti estai ekeinōi hōi an genētai t'agatha; tout' euporōteron, ēn d' egō, echō apokrinasthai, hoti eudaimōn estai. The participle metabalōn (having changed) explicitly marks the substitution; anti tou kalou tōi agathōi (instead of the beautiful, using the good) specifies what is being replaced. The contrast with the earlier aporia is marked by the comparative euporōteron (more resourcefully)."

It does not assess whether the substitution is valid, whether it resolves the underlying philosophical question, or whether eudaimōn is a satisfactory answer. It does not claim that this substitution prefigures later moves in Diotima's speech."
