The explanation accounts for why not everyone is said to love, even though all desire good things."
textual_basis: "At 205a-205b, after Socrates expresses wonder at the discrepancy, Diotima says: 'Do not wonder. For we separate off one form of eros and apply the name of the whole to it, calling it eros, while we use other names for the other forms.' The verb aphilontes (separating off) and the noun eidos (form/species) mark the selection of one part; the phrase to tou holou onoma (the name of the whole) marks the part-for-whole naming convention; the verb katachrōmetha (we use/abuse) marks the application of other names to the remaining forms."
limits: "This observation records the part-for-whole naming explanation at 205a-205b. It does not assess whether this linguistic convention is arbitrary or motivated, whether it resolves the philosophical tension about eros's scope, or whether the eidos language anticipates the Forms. It does not claim that this naming convention is unique to eros."

```yaml
observation_id: obs_symposium_0114
source_work: Symposium

  - ποίησίς
  - ἐκ τοῦ μὴ ὄντος εἰς τὸ ὂν ἰόντι

  - μουσικῇ

english_gloss: ""
feature_family: craft_analogy
feature_id: feature_candidate_233
feature_label: analogy_for_naming_convention
observation: "At 205b, Diotima illustrates the part-for-whole naming convention with the analogy of poiesis (making/production). Poiesis is something manifold (ti poly): any cause for anything going from not-being to being is poiesis, so all crafts are poiesis and all craftsmen are poets. But we do not call them all poets; we separate off one part of poiesis concerning music and meter and call only that poiesis. The analogy maps the general/specific naming structure of eros onto the general/specific naming structure of poiesis."
textual_basis: "At 205b, when Socrates asks for an example, Diotima says: 'Like this. Do you know that poiesis is something manifold? For any cause for anything going from not-being to being is poiesis, so all the works of all crafts are poiesis and all their craftsmen are poets.' She then states the restriction: 'But as you know, we do not call them all poets, but we separate off one part of poiesis concerning music and meter, and call only that by the name of the whole.' The analogy parallels the eros case: a general category (all making / all desire for good) is narrowed by convention to a specific form (metered composition / sexual-romantic eros)."
limits: "This observation records the poiesis analogy as an illustrative device within Diotima's speech at 205b. It does not assess whether the analogy is valid, whether the definition of poiesis as any cause of coming-into-being is philosophically significant, or whether the analogy implies that eros-as-desire-for-good is the true general category and sexual eros merely a conventional narrowing. It does not claim that the poiesis definition anticipates later Platonic metaphysics."
review_status: accepted
```

For only this is called poiesis.' At 205d, she applies the analogy: 'Thus also concerning eros. The chief point is that all desire for good things and happiness is the greatest and most treacherous eros for everyone; but those turning elsewhere in many ways toward it, whether to money-making or to love of gymnastics or to philosophy, are not called lovers nor erastai, while those going after one specific form and being zealous hold the name of the whole — eros and eran and erastai.' The explicit connective 'houtō toinyn kai peri ton erōta' (thus also concerning eros) marks the application."

It does not assess whether the analogy is valid, whether the naming convention is arbitrary or motivated, or whether the specific form (hen ti eidos) refers to pederastic eros specifically. The list places philosophy alongside money-making and gymnastics as pursuits that are forms of the universal desire for good but are not given the name eros. Only those who pursue one specific form (hen ti eidos) with zeal receive the name of the whole."
textual_basis: "At 205d, Diotima says: 'all desire for good things and happiness is the greatest and most treacherous eros for everyone; but those turning elsewhere in many ways toward it, whether to money-making (chrēmatismos) or to love of gymnastics (philogymnastia) or to philosophy (philosophia), are not called lovers nor erastai, while those going after one specific form and being zealous hold the name of the whole — eros and eran and erastai.' The three pursuits are listed in a parallel construction (ē kata ... ē kata ... ē kata), and philosophy is the third item in the list."

It does not claim that Diotima is denigrating philosophy by listing it alongside money-making and gymnastics."

feature_id: feature_candidate_234
feature_label: definition_refined_by_successive_addition
observation: "At 206a, Diotima (through Socrates's reported speech) builds the definition of eros through three successive refinements, each elicited as an addition (prostetheon) to the previous one. First, humans love the good (t'agathou erōsin). Second, they love that the good be theirs (einai to agathon hautois). Third, they love that the good be theirs always (aei einai). The final summary definition states: 'Eros is, in sum, the love of the good being one's own always' (estin ara syllēbdēn ho erōs tou to agathon hautōi einai aei)."
textual_basis: "At 206a, the text reports three questions and three agreements. First: 'Is it so simple to say that humans love the good?' — 'Yes.' Second: 'Must we not add (prostetheon) that they love the good to be theirs?' — 'We must add it.' Third: 'And not only to be, but also to be always?' — 'We must add this too.' The conclusion follows: 'Eros is, in sum, the love of the good being one's own always.' The term prostetheon (must be added) is repeated for each refinement, marking the additive structure explicitly."
limits: "This observation records the additive refinement structure and the resulting definition at 206a. It does not assess whether this definition is consistent with earlier definitions of eros in the dialogue, whether it is the final or complete definition Diotima offers, or whether the successive additions are logically entailed by the previous steps. It does not claim that this definition resolves all tensions in the dialogue's treatment of eros."
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: "At 206a, the text reports: Diotima asks 'Is it so simple to say that humans love the good?' Socrates answers 'Yes' (nai). This moves from defining what Eros desires to defining the activity through which that desire is pursued. Socrates responds that he does not understand and requires clearer explanation (206b)."
textual_basis: "At 206b, Diotima asks: since Eros is always this (desire for good to be one's own always), in what manner and in what action (praxis) would the pursuit and intensity be called Eros? What is its ergon? She answers: 'This is tokos en kalōi, both in body and in soul' (esti gar touto tokos en kalōi kai kata to sōma kai kata tēn psychēn). Socrates responds that he does not understand (ou manthanō), prompting Diotima to explain more clearly at 206c."
limits: "This observation records the definition of Eros's ergon as tokos en kalōi at 206b. It does not assess whether this definition is consistent with the earlier definition of Eros as desire for the good, whether the body/soul distinction is exhaustive, or whether Socrates's incomprehension is genuine or feigned. It does not claim that this is Diotima's final or complete account of Eros."
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "At 206c, Diotima grounds the ergon of Eros in a universal anthropological claim: all humans are pregnant (kyousin) in body and soul, and when they reach a certain age, nature desires to give birth (tiktein). Birth cannot occur in ugliness (aischrōi) but only in beauty (kalōi). The union of man and woman is birth. This is described as a divine thing (theion pragma), and in the mortal living being there is something immortal — pregnancy and birth (kyēsis kai gennēsis). These cannot happen in what is discordant/anarmostōi."
textual_basis: "At 206c, Diotima says: 'All humans are pregnant (kyousin), Socrates, both in body and in soul, and when they reach a certain age, our nature desires to give birth (tiktein). To give birth in ugliness is not possible, but in beauty. For the union of man and woman is birth. This is a divine thing, and this in a mortal living being is something immortal — pregnancy and birth. These cannot happen in what is discordant.' The universal quantifier pantes anthrōpoi (all humans) and the body/soul pairing establish the scope. The kalon/aischron contrast and the theion/athanaton characterization provide the evaluative and metaphysical framing."
limits: "This observation records the universal pregnancy claim and the beauty-requirement for birth at 206c. It does not assess whether the claim is biologically or philosophically sound, whether the immortal element in mortal beings refers to the species or to something else, or whether this claim is consistent with the later ascent passage. It does not claim that this is a complete theory of reproduction or that it represents Plato's considered view."
review_status: accepted
```

When Socrates says Eros is of the beautiful, Diotima states: 'Eros is not of the beautiful, as you think, Socrates, but of birth and procreation in the beautiful.' This is a definitional refinement that shifts the object of eros from the beautiful itself to the activity of generation within the beautiful. Socrates accepts the correction with 'eien' (let it be)."
textual_basis: "At 206e, the text reads: 'estin gar, ō Sōkrates, ephē, ou tou kalou ho erōs, hōs sy oiei.' — 'alla ti mēn;' — 'tēs gennēseōs kai tou tokou en tōi kalōi.' Socrates responds: 'eien, ēn d' egō.' The correction is explicit: the genitive object shifts from tou kalou (of the beautiful) to tēs gennēseōs kai tou tokou en tōi kalōi (of birth and procreation in the beautiful). The phrase 'hōs sy oiei' marks the correction as a revision of Socrates's prior understanding."
limits: "This observation records the explicit correction of the object of eros at 206e. It does not assess whether this correction is consistent with the earlier definition of eros as desire for the good to be one's own always, whether it resolves the tension between the two formulations, or whether Socrates's 'eien' indicates genuine acceptance or polite acknowledgment. It does not claim that this is the final definitional step in Diotima's speech."

Beauty is personified as a divine agent that enables birth."

The ugly is stated to be discordant (anarmoston) with the divine, the beautiful harmonious (harmotton). From this, Beauty (Kallone) is identified as both Moira (Fate, the apportioner) and Eileithyia (the birth-goddess) for generation (genesis). The identification is presented as a conclusion from the harmony premise."
limits: "This observation records the identification of Beauty as Moira and Eileithyia for generation at 206d. It does not assess whether this identification is meant as literal theology, mythic personification, or metaphorical claim. feature_label: biological_grounding_of_eros
observation: "At 206d-206e, Diotima describes the physiological phenomenology of the pregnant organism's response to beauty versus ugliness. When the pregnant (to kyoun) approaches the beautiful, it becomes gracious (hileon), rejoices, flows (diacheitai), and gives birth (tiktei). When it approaches the ugly, it becomes sullen (skythrōpon), pained, contracts (syspeiratai), turns away (apotrepeta), and does not give birth. The great flutter (ptoiēsis) about the beautiful arises because it releases the one having it from great birth-pangs (ōdinos)."
textual_basis: "At 206d-206e, the text reads: 'dia tauta hotan men kalōi prospelazēi to kyoun, hileon te gignetai kai euphrainomenon diacheitai kai tiktei te kai gennai; hotan de aischrōi, skythrōpon te kai lygoumenon syspeiratai kai apotrepeta kai aneilletai kai ou gennai, alla ischon to kyēma chalepōs pherei. hothen dē tōi kyounti te kai ēdē spargōnti pollē hē ptoiēsis gegone peri to kalon dia to megalēs ōdinos apolyein ton echonta.' The sequence of verbs describes the contrasting physiological responses: hileon (gracious/cheerful), diacheitai (flows/dissolves), tiktei (gives birth) vs. skythrōpon (sullen), syspeiratai (contracts), apotrepeta (turns away), aneilletai (is coiled up). The ptoiēsis (flutter/agitation) is explained as arising from the release from birth-pangs."
limits: "This observation records the physiological phenomenology described at 206d-206e. It does not assess whether this description is meant literally or metaphorically, whether it is consistent with Greek medical theories of reproduction, or whether the ptoiēsis explanation is intended as a complete account of why humans pursue beauty. It does not claim that this phenomenology anticipates or contradicts the later ascent passage."
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "At 207a-207b, Diotima grounds the claim that eros is of immortality in observable animal behavior. She asks Socrates what cause (aition) drives all beasts — both land and winged — when they desire to procreate. They become sick (nosounta) and erotically disposed (erotikos diatithemena), first seeking to mingle, then caring for offspring, ready to fight the strongest and die for them (hyperapothneskein), and starving themselves to feed their young. The passage contrasts this with humans, who might be thought to act from calculation (ek logismou), while the cause for beasts is left as a question."
textual_basis: "At 207a-207b, the text reads: 'ti oiei, o Sokrates, aition einai toutou tou erotos kai tes epithymias; e ouk aisthanei hos deinos diatithetai panta ta theria epeidan gennan epithymese, kai ta peza kai ta ptena, nosounta te panta kai erotikos diatithemena, proton men peri to symmigenai allelois, epeita peri ten trophen tou genomenou, kai hetoima estin hyper touton kai diamachesthai ta asthenestata tois ischyrotatois kai hyperapothneskein, kai auta toi limoi parateinomena host' ekeina ektrephein, kai allo pan poiounta.' The sequence of behaviors (mingling, feeding, fighting, dying, starving) is presented as evidence of an eros-driven impulse toward reproduction that transcends individual survival. The contrast with humans (tous men gar anthropous ... ek logismou) marks the animal evidence as more purely instinctual."
limits: "This observation records the animal behavior evidence Diotima presents at 207a-207b. It does not assess whether the animal evidence successfully proves that eros is of immortality, whether the contrast with human calculation is exhaustive, or whether the animal behaviors described are accurately observed. It does not claim that this argument is the final or complete justification for the immortality thesis."
review_status: accepted

observation: "At 207d-207e, Diotima argues that a living being is conventionally called the same person from childhood to old age despite never having the same constituents in itself — always becoming new in some respects and losing in others, regarding hair, flesh, bones, blood, and the whole body. She then extends the same replacement principle from body to soul: characters, habits, opinions, desires, pleasures, pains, fears never remain the same but some come to be, others perish."
textual_basis: "At 207d, Diotima states: 'each of the animals is called to live and be the same... from a child the same is called until he becomes old; this one, however, never having the same things in himself, is nevertheless called the same, but always becoming new, losing things, and regarding hair and flesh and bones and blood and the whole body.' At 207e, she extends: 'and not only regarding the body, but also regarding the soul — characters, habits, opinions, desires, pleasures, pains, fears — each of these is never present as the same to each, but some come to be, others perish.' The contrast between conventional naming (ho autos kaleitai) and actual flux (oude pote ta auta echōn en hautō) is the core textual phenomenon."
limits: "This observation records the identity paradox and the body-to-soul extension of the flux principle at 207d-207e. It does not assess whether conventional identity naming is philosophically justified, whether the flux of psychological states is conceptually parallel to material flux, or whether this argument successfully establishes generation as the mechanism of immortality. The observation does not cover the continuation about knowledge (epistēmai) at the end of 207e, which appears in the text but is truncated in this segment."
review_status: accepted

feature_label: identity_persistence_through_flux
observation: At 208a, Diotima extends the flux principle to knowledge (epistēmai), stating that not only are humans never the same even regarding their knowledges, but each individual knowledge undergoes the same process — some arise, others perish. She specifies the mechanism: what is called study (meletan) exists because knowledge departs; forgetfulness (lēthē) is the departure of knowledge; study produces new memory in place of the departing one, preserving the knowledge so that it appears to be the same.
textual_basis: At 208a, the text states: "and never are we the same even with respect to our knowledges, but each individual knowledge undergoes the same thing. For what is called studying is because knowledge departs; forgetfulness is the departure of knowledge, and study producing new memory in place of the one departing preserves the knowledge, so that it appears to be the same." The universal claim (oude pote hoi autoi esmen oude kata tas epistēmas) and the specific mechanism of memory replacement constitute the textual basis.
limits: This observation records the extension of the flux principle to knowledge and the memory-replacement mechanism at 208a. It does not assess whether this theory of knowledge-identity through study is philosophically sound, whether it conflicts with other Platonic passages on knowledge, or whether it is consistent with the earlier definition of eros as desire for permanent possession of the good.
review_status: accepted
---
```

english_gloss: ""

observation: "At 208c-208e, Diotima extends the argument that eros is of immortality from the biological domain to the civic domain of love of honor (philotimia). She argues that humans are terribly disposed by eros to become famous and lay down immortal glory (208c), willing to risk dangers, spend money, endure labors, and die for this more than for their own children. The conclusion at 208e states that everyone does everything for immortal virtue and glorious reputation, and the better they are, the more so — 'for they love the immortal' (tou gar athanatou erōsin). This extends the evidence base for the claim that eros is of immortality from biological procreation to social ambition for posthumous fame."
textual_basis: "At 208c, Diotima says: 'if you are willing to look at the love of honor (philotimia) among humans, you would wonder at its irrationality... how terribly they are disposed by eros to become famous and lay down immortal glory forever, and for this they are ready to risk all dangers even more than for their children, and to spend money and endure any labors and die for it.' At 208d, she asks whether Alcestis, Achilles, and Codrus would have died without believing they would have immortal memory of their virtue. At 208e, she concludes: 'everyone does everything for immortal virtue and such glorious reputation, and the better they are, the more so; for they love the immortal.' The philotimia argument is presented as a new domain of evidence for the same conclusion about eros and immortality."
limits: "This observation records the philotimia argument as an extension of the evidence base for the claim that eros is of immortality. It does not assess whether the argument is valid, whether the inference from love of honor to desire for immortality is sound, or whether this extension is consistent with the earlier biological argument. It does not claim that this is the final or complete argument in Diotima's speech."
review_status: accepted
```

feature_label: triadic_mythic_exemplum_chain_with_ranking
observation: "At 208d, Diotima deploys a triadic chain of mythic exempla — Alcestis, Achilles, and Codrus — as evidence that people act for immortal memory of virtue. Alcestis died for Admetus, Achilles died after Patroclus, and Codrus died before his children for the kingdom. The three are presented as parallel cases: all believed they would have immortal memory of their virtue, which we now possess. Unlike the ranked chain in Phaedrus's speech, Diotima does not contrast them with a negative exemplar; all three are positive evidence for the same claim."
textual_basis: "At 208d, Diotima asks: 'Do you think Alcestis would have died for Admetus, or Achilles would have died after Patroclus, or your Codrus would have died before his children for the kingdom, if they did not believe there would be immortal memory of their virtue, which we now possess? Far from it... but I think everyone does everything for immortal virtue and such glorious reputation.' The three names are listed in a parallel interrogative construction (ē ... ē ... ē), each with a specific heroic action (dying for spouse, dying after beloved, dying before children for kingdom). The shared motive is the belief in immortal memory of virtue."
limits: "This observation records the triadic exemplum structure at 208d. It does not assess whether the exempla are used accurately to their traditional forms, whether the absence of a negative exemplar distinguishes this from other uses of similar figures in the dialogue, or whether the argument from mythic examples is rhetorically or philosophically sound. It does not claim that the exempla are original to Diotima."
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "At 208e, Diotima introduces a bifurcation between those pregnant in body (hoi men enkymones kata ta sōmata) and those pregnant in soul (hoi de kata tēn psychēn, implied continuation). Those pregnant in body turn to women and are erotic through childbearing (paidogonia), providing themselves immortality, memory, and happiness for all future time, as they think. This marks the transition from the general argument about eros and immortality to a specific distinction between two types of procreative pursuit."
textual_basis: "At 208e, the text reads: 'Those pregnant in body turn rather to women and are erotic in this way, through childbearing providing themselves immortality and memory and happiness, as they think, for all future time.' The men ... de construction introduces the bifurcation: the first branch (those pregnant in body) is described; the second branch (those pregnant in soul) is introduced but the text of the segment cuts off before its description. The three goods sought through childbearing are listed: immortality (athanasia), memory (mnēmē), and happiness (eudaimonia)."
limits: "This observation records the introduction of the body-pregnant/soul-pregnant bifurcation at 208e. It does not assess whether the bifurcation is exhaustive, whether the three goods (immortality, memory, happiness) are consistent with the earlier definition of eros, or whether the soul-pregnant branch will be described as superior. The observation covers only what is within the cited span; the continuation describing soul-pregnancy is outside this segment."

Poets (poiētai) are named as generators (gennētores) of these, along with craftsmen (dēmiourgoi) who are said to be inventive (heuretikoi). The pregnancy/birth framework previously applied to bodily procreation is extended to intellectual and artistic production."

The verb kyousin (are pregnant) and the nouns gennētores (generators) extend the biological vocabulary of the earlier argument to the intellectual domain. The list of generators (poets, inventive craftsmen) specifies who produces wisdom and virtue."
limits: "This observation records the extension of the pregnancy/birth framework to soul-pregnancy and the identification of poets and craftsmen as generators. It does not assess whether the soul-pregnancy framework is consistent with the earlier biological argument, whether poets and craftsmen exhaust the category of soul-pregnant people, or whether this extension is meant to elevate or demote intellectual production relative to biological procreation."

He welcomes beautiful bodies, but when he meets a beautiful, noble, and well-natured soul (psychē kalē kai gennaia kai euphuēs), he welcomes the combination (to synamphoteron) and immediately becomes resourceful in speeches (euporei logōn) about virtue and about what sort of man one should be."

So he welcomes beautiful bodies more than ugly ones, being pregnant, and if he meets a beautiful and noble and well-natured soul, he greatly welcomes the combination, and toward this man he immediately becomes resourceful in speeches about virtue and about what sort of man one should be.' The sequence moves from desire (epithymēi) to search (zētei) to welcome (aspazetai) to resourcefulness (euporei logōn). The three soul-adjectives (kalē, gennaia, euphuēs) specify the conditions for the full response."
limits: "This observation records the behavioral sequence described at 209a-209b. It does not assess whether this description is meant as a normative ideal, whether the combination of beautiful body and noble soul is necessary or merely sufficient for the response, or whether the resourcefulness in speeches about virtue is the primary or exclusive outcome of the encounter. It does not claim that this passage anticipates the ascent passage in Diotima's speech."

This explicit claim of the unity of beauty across particular bodies grounds the transition from erotic attachment to one beautiful body to becoming a lover of all beautiful bodies."
textual_basis: "At 210a-210b, the text reads: 'to kallos to epi hotoioun somati toi epi heteroi somati adelphon esti, kai ei dei diokein to ep' eidei kalon, polle anoia me ouch hen te kai tauton hegeisthai to epi pasin tois somasi kallos.' Three elements are present: (1) the sibling relation (adelphon) between beauties on different bodies, (2) the phrase 'to ep' eidei kalon' (the beautiful in form/species) which introduces form-language, and (3) the evaluative claim that failure to recognize beauty's unity is great folly (polle anoia)."
limits: "This observation records the claim of beauty's unity across bodies and the form-language at 210a-210b. It does not assess whether this claim successfully establishes the existence of a single Form of Beauty, whether the sibling relation implies numerical or qualitative identity, or whether the argument from 'ei dei diokein to ep' eidei kalon' is valid as a proof of unity."
review_status: accepted
```

feature_label: ordered_ascent_steps
observation: "Diotima outlines an ordered sequence of stages in erotic education continuing from the recognition of beauty across bodies and soul-beauty: (1) being compelled to contemplate the beauty in practices and laws and see it as all akin to itself, so that bodily beauty seems small; (2) being led to the sciences to see the beauty of sciences; (3) looking at the vast sea of beauty, giving birth to many beautiful and magnificent speeches and thoughts in ungrudging philosophy; (4) strengthened and increased there, catching sight of a single science which is of a certain beauty. Each stage is marked by an explicit transitional marker and reorients the erotic impulse toward a more universal object."
textual_basis: "At 210c, after valuing soul-beauty above body-beauty, the learner is compelled to see the beauty in practices and laws (to en tois epitedeumasi kai tois nomois kalon) and see that all of it is akin to itself (pān auto hautōi syngenes estin), so that he considers bodily beauty a small thing. At 210c-210d, after practices, he is led to the sciences (epi tas epistēmas) to see the beauty of sciences. At 210d, looking at the vast sea of the beautiful (epi to poly pelagos tou kalou), he gives birth to many beautiful speeches and thoughts in ungrudging philosophy (tiktei ... logous ... kai dianoēmata en philosophiai aphthonōi). At 210d-210e, strengthened and increased there (entautha rhōstheis kai auxētheis), he catches sight of a single science (katidēi tina epistēmēn mian) of such beauty. The transitional phrases meta de tauta, meta de ta epitedeumata, and heōs an mark the sequence."
limits: "This observation records the ordered sequential structure of the ascent stages within 210c-210e and their transitional markers. It does not assess whether the sequence is exhaustive, whether each stage is logically entailed by the previous one, whether this ladder structure is original to Plato or adapted from other traditions, or whether the stages represent a unified philosophical program. It does not claim that the 'single science' at 210d-210e is identical to the vision at 210e."
review_status: accepted
---

greek_terms:
  - ἀεὶ ὂν

feature_label: form_properties_catalogue
observation: "At 211a-211b, Beauty itself is described through a series of negative predications: it always is, not coming-to-be nor passing away, not increasing nor decreasing, not beautiful in one way and ugly in another, nor at one time beautiful and another not, nor in one relation beautiful and in another ugly, nor here beautiful and there ugly, nor seeming beautiful to some and ugly to others. It does not appear as a face or hands or anything bodily, nor as any account or knowledge, nor as being in anything else. Positively, it is itself by itself with itself, uniform, always being."
textual_basis: "At 211a, the text opens with the negative series: always being, neither coming-to-be nor passing away, neither increasing nor decreasing, followed by a series of negated relativities: not beautiful in one way and ugly in another, nor at one time beautiful and another not, nor in one relation beautiful and in another ugly, nor here beautiful and there ugly, nor seeming beautiful to some and ugly to others. At 211a-211b, further negations: it does not appear as a face or hands or anything bodily, nor as any account or knowledge, nor as being in something else. At 211b, the positive description: itself by itself, with itself, uniform, always being (auto kath' hauto meth' hautou monoeides aei on). This is within Diotima's reported speech describing the final vision of the erotic ascent."
limits: "This observation records the negative/positive descriptive structure of Beauty itself as presented in the text at 211a-211b. It does not assess whether this description constitutes a metaphysical doctrine, whether the negative predications are exhaustive, or whether the description is original to Plato or derivative. It does not claim that this passage proves the existence of Forms in other Platonic dialogues."
review_status: accepted
```

The text further specifies that while the other beautiful things come to be and pass away, the Form itself does not become any greater or lesser nor suffer anything."
textual_basis: "At 211b, after the apophatic description of Beauty itself, the text reads: all other beautiful things participate in that (Form) in a certain way (ekeinou metechonta tropon tina toiouton), such that while the others come to be and pass away, that Form becomes neither any greater nor any lesser nor suffers anything. limits: "This observation records the use of methexo (participation) vocabulary and the asymmetry of change between the Form and its participants at 211b. It does not assess whether this participation theory is coherent, whether it anticipates or differs from participation language in other Platonic dialogues, or whether the tropon tina qualification signals a problem with the participation relation."

observation: "At 211b, after the description of the Form of Beauty, the text states that when someone, ascends from these things through correct pederasty and begins to catch sight of that Beauty, he would almost touch the end (schedon an ti haptoito tou telous). This is the climax statement of the erotic ascent, marking the moment when the upward journey reaches its goal."
textual_basis: "At 211b, the text reads: hotan de tis apo tonde dia to orthos paiderastein epanion ekeino to kalon archesthai kathoran, schedon an ti haptoito tou telous. The verb epanion (going up/ascending from) marks the upward movement; kathoran (to see clearly, catch sight of) marks the visual/intellectual apprehension; haptoito tou telous (touch the end) marks the attainment of the goal. The qualification schedon (almost, nearly) and the phrase dia to orthos paiderastein (through correct pederasty) specify the means and the proximity to the goal."
limits: "This observation records the culmination statement as a textual event at 211b. It does not assess whether the telos (end/goal) is the Form itself or the vision of it, whether schedon (almost) implies the goal is never fully attained, or whether orthos paiderastein is a direct reference to Pausanias's earlier speech. It does not claim that this statement resolves the definition of eros in the dialogue."
review_status: accepted
```

feature_id: feature_candidate_246
feature_label: ascent_culmination
observation: At 211d-211e, Diotima makes two culminating claims about the vision of Beauty itself: (1) this is where life is worth living for a human being, contemplating Beauty itself; (2) the hypothetical that if someone could see Beauty itself pure, clear, unmixed, not filled with human flesh and colors and mortal nonsense, but divine and uniform, this would be the ultimate attainment. The passage contrasts ordinary erotic fascination (being willing to abandon food and drink to gaze upon a beautiful beloved) with the contemplation of Beauty itself.
textual_basis: At 211d, the Mantinean woman says this is where if anywhere a human being should live, contemplating Beauty itself. At 211d-211e, the hypothetical is posed: if someone could see Beauty itself pure, clear, unmixed, not filled with human flesh and colors and much other mortal nonsense, but could behold the divine beauty itself, uniform. The contrast with conventional erotic fixation is drawn at 211d by mentioning the beautiful boys and youths for whom one would give up eating and drinking to gaze upon and be with them. The words for 'contemplating,' 'to see,' and 'to behold' mark visual/intellectual apprehension as the goal.
limits: This observation records the culminating claims at 211d-211e. It does not assess whether these claims are meant literally or rhetorically, whether the vision is attainable in human life or only after death, or whether the description of contemplating Beauty itself is consistent with the earlier stages of the ascent. It does not interpret the significance of the direct address to Socrates by name.
review_status: accepted
```

The person who sees Beauty itself and touches the truth (tou alēthous ephaptomenos) generates true virtue (alēthē aretēn), not images of virtue (eidōla aretēs) as those who only touch an image (eidōlou ephaptomenos) do. The contrast between the two types of production is grounded in whether the person contacts the truth itself or only an image."
textual_basis: "At 212a, the text contrasts touching an image (ou gar eidōlou ephaptomenos) with touching the truth (tou alēthous ephaptomenos), and pairs each with its product: eidōla aretēs (images of virtue) vs alēthē aretēn (true virtue). The contrast is presented as the consequence of seeing Beauty itself (horōnti hōi horaton to kalon). The verb tiktein (to give birth) is used for both types of production."
limits: "This observation records the image/truth contrast in the outcome of the vision of Beauty itself. It does not assess whether the distinction between images and truth maps onto a metaphysical theory of Forms in other dialogues, whether only the philosopher can produce true virtue, or whether eidōla aretēs refers to conventional or civic virtue. It does not claim that this is the final or complete statement of Diotima's teaching about virtue production."

feature_family: closure_type
feature_id: feature_candidate_1214
feature_label: encomium_closing_formula
observation: "Socrates closes his encomium by stating his personal conviction in Diotima's teaching and delivering a universal exhortation. He declares that Diotima said these things but he himself is persuaded, and being persuaded he tries to persuade others that no better helper for human nature than Eros could be found. He urges every man to honor Eros, states that he himself honors and practices erotic matters especially, and says he always praises the power and courage of Eros."
textual_basis: "At 212b, Socrates addresses Phaedrus and the others: Diotima said these things, but I am persuaded; being persuaded I try to persuade others that for human nature one could not easily find a better helper than Eros; therefore I say every man must honor Eros, and I myself honor erotic matters and practice them especially, and urge others, and now and always I praise the power and courage of Eros as far as I am able."
limits: "This observation records the personal conviction statement and exhortation as a speech-closing structure at 212b. It does not assess whether the conviction is genuine or ironic, whether the exhortation is meant seriously, or whether this closing completes or undermines the philosophical content of Diotima's speech."
review_status: accepted
```

limits: "This observation records the disruption as a dramatic event within the narrative at 212c. It does not identify the komasts beyond what is stated in the text at this span, does not interpret the significance of the aulos-girl or the komos, and does not assess whether the interruption is accidental or dramatically motivated."

feature_label: komast_arrival_disruption
observation: Alcibiades's first words upon entering propose two alternative terms for his admission: either the company accepts him as a fellow drinking companion (sympotēn) despite being very drunk, or he and his companions will crown Agathon and depart. The proposal is presented as a question and frames his presence as conditional on the group's acceptance.
textual_basis: At 212e, Alcibiades is reported to have said: 'Men, greetings: will you accept a very drunk man as a drinking companion, or should we go away after crowning Agathon, which is why we came?' The phrase 'dexesthe sympotēn' (accept as a fellow drinker) and the alternative 'apiōmen anadēsantes monon Agathōna' (should we go away after only crowning Agathon) present a binary choice that establishes the terms under which Alcibiades enters the symposium.
limits: This observation records the proposed terms of entry as stated at 212e. It does not assess whether the proposal is sincere or a rhetorical strategy, does not interpret the significance of crowning Agathon, and does not claim that the binary choice is the actual basis on which Alcibiades is admitted.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: At 213a, Alcibiades says to the laughing company: "You laugh, but I know well that I speak the truth. But tell me now: on fixed terms do I enter or not? Will you drink with me or not?" The adversative conjunction links the truth-claim to the anticipated mockery; the phrase "on fixed terms" uses a legal/commercial metaphor for conditional admission.
limits: This observation records the statement as a textual event at 213a. It does not assess whether Alcibiades's truth-claim is justified, does not interpret the content of what he asserts is true, and does not claim that the mockery is actual rather than anticipated.

greek_terms:
  - ἐπαμύνεις
  - ἔρως
  - οὐ φαῦλον πρᾶγμα
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: dramatic_case_setup
feature_id: feature_candidate_1219
feature_label: symposium_erotic_rivalry_frame
observation: "At 213c, Alcibiades turns to Agathon and asks for defense against Socrates, declaring that Socrates's eros for him (Alcibiades) has been no small matter. The passage shifts from Alcibiades's direct address to Socrates to an appeal to Agathon, and explicitly characterizes the Socrates-Alcibiades relationship as a significant erotic history."
textual_basis: "At 213c, after questioning Socrates's seating choice, Alcibiades says to Agathon: 'See whether you defend me (epamynnis), for this man's love (eros) for me has been no small matter (ou phaulon pragma).' The verb epamynnis (defend, come to aid) frames Alcibiades as needing protection from Socrates; the noun eros and the litotes ou phaulon pragma (not a trivial matter) mark the relationship as significant and erotic."
limits: "This observation records Alcibiades's appeal to Agathon and his characterization of Socrates's eros. It does not assess whether the claim is accurate, whether the defense appeal is earnest or playful, or whether this sets up the content of Alcibiades's later encomium speech. It does not claim that 'ou phaulon pragma' has specific content beyond its stated meaning."
review_status: accepted
```

observation: At 213d, Alcibiades, speaking to Agathon, states that since he fell in love (with Agathon), he cannot look at or converse with any beautiful person without Socrates becoming jealous and envious, working wonders, insulting him, and barely restraining his hands. Alcibiades asks Agathon to reconcile them or to defend him if Socrates tries to use force, stating that he dreads Socrates's madness and love-passion.
textual_basis: At 213d, the reported speech of Alcibiades contains: 'since I fell in love, it is no longer possible for me to look at or converse with any beautiful person at all, or this man (Socrates) being jealous and envious works wonders and insults me and barely keeps his hands off. See that he does not do something even now, but reconcile us, or if he tries to use force, defend me, for I greatly dread this man's madness and love-passion.'
limits: This observation records Alcibiades's claims and requests as reported in the text. It does not assess whether the claims about Socrates's behavior are accurate, whether Alcibiades's fear is genuine, or whether the request for reconciliation or defense is sincere. It does not interpret the significance of philerastia.
review_status: accepted
```

feature_id: feature_candidate_1220
feature_label: socratic_erotic_role_reversal_profile
observation: At 213d, Alcibiades provides a catalogue of specific behavioral descriptors for Socrates's erotic possessiveness: being jealous (zelotypōn), envying (phthonōn), working wonders (thaumasta ergazetai), insulting (loidoreitai), and barely restraining his hands (tō cheire mogis apechetai). Alcibiades further names Socrates's motivating drives as madness (mania) and love-passion (philerastia), which he states he greatly dreads.
textual_basis: At 213d, the text presents a sequence of participles and finite verbs describing Socrates's behavior: zelotypōn (being jealous), phthonōn (envying), thaumasta ergazetai (works wonders), loidoreitai (insults), tō cheire mogis apechetai (barely keeps his hands off). The passage concludes with the two nouns mania (madness) and philerastia (love-passion) which Alcibiades says he dreads (orthōdei). All seven terms are present in the passage as descriptors attributed to Socrates by Alcibiades.
limits: This observation records the specific descriptors Alcibiades applies to Socrates at 213d. It does not assess whether the description is accurate, whether it is consistent with other characterizations of Socrates in the dialogue or elsewhere, or whether the catalogue is complete. It does not claim that these descriptors constitute a formal or exhaustive portrait.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: "At 213e, Alcibiades is reported to have said: 'Give me some of the fillets, Agathon, so that I may also crown this man's wonderful head, and let him not find fault with me because I crowned you but did not crown him, who wins in speeches over all people, not just recently like you but always.' observation: "After reclining, Alcibiades declares that the company seems to him to be sober, states this cannot be permitted and that they must drink since they agreed to it. textual_basis: "At 213e, after reclining, Alcibiades is reported to have said: 'Well then, men, you seem to me to be sober. This cannot be permitted, you must drink; for we agreed to this. Therefore I choose myself as ruler of the drinking until you have drunk sufficiently. Agathon, have a large drinking cup brought. Or rather, no need — boy, bring me that cooler.' The procedural vocabulary includes forms indicating necessity (must be permitted, must drink), self-appointment (I choose as ruler), and the call for a large vessel."
limits: "This observation records the procedural takeover of drinking authority. It does not assess whether Alcibiades is entitled to this role, whether the company agreed to heavy drinking, or whether this move is meant as parody of Eryximachus's earlier procedural consolidation. It does not compare this to other instances of symposiarch appointment in the dialogue."
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: "At 214b, the text reports Alcibiades saying to Eryximachus: 'I must obey you; for a doctor is worth many other men. Command therefore whatever you wish.' The imperative 'command' (epitatte) follows from the stated submission to medical authority, and the Homeric citation serves as the warrant for that submission."
limits: "This observation records the Homeric quotation and the deference statement. observation: "At 214c, Eryximachus informs Alcibiades of the encomium plan that was established before his arrival: each person in turn from left to right must give the finest possible speech praising Eros. He states that everyone else has already spoken, and that since Alcibiades has not yet spoken and has drunk, he must speak; after speaking, he may command Socrates to do whatever he wishes, and then the next to the right, proceeding in order."
textual_basis: "At 214c, Eryximachus reports: 'Before you entered, we decided that each of us in turn from left to right should give a speech about Eros as finely as possible and praise him. All the rest of us have spoken. Since you have not spoken and have drunk, it is just that you speak, and after speaking, command Socrates to do whatever you wish, and he the next to the right, and so the rest.' The procedural terms (en merei, epi dexia, eirekamen, ouk eirekas, ekpepokas, dikaois ei eipein, epitaxai) articulate a complete set of rules for this round of the symposium."
limits: "This observation records the encomium plan as communicated to Alcibiades at 214c. It does not assess whether this plan was genuinely established earlier, whether Alcibiades is bound by it, or whether the speaking order will be followed."
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "At 215a, Alcibiades announces he will praise Socrates through images (di' eikonōn). He hedges that Socrates may think the images tend toward the ridiculous, but states that the image is for the sake of truth, not the ridiculous. The announcement preemptively addresses the suspicion that the comparison to Silenus statues and satyrs is comic mockery rather than truth-telling characterization."
textual_basis: "At 215a, Alcibiades says: 'I will attempt to praise Socrates thus, through images (di' eikonōn). He may think it tends toward the ridiculous (epi ta geloiotera), but the image is for the sake of truth (tou alēthous heneka), not the ridiculous (ou tou geloiou).' The men ... de contrast between how Socrates will receive it (geloiotera) and the actual purpose (alēthous heneka) marks the hedge explicitly."
limits: "This observation records the announcement and the truth-vs-ridicule hedge. It does not assess whether the subsequent Silenus/Marsyas comparison is actually for truth or mockery, whether Alcibiades is being sincere or ironic, or whether the hedge is a rhetorical convention."
review_status: accepted

When opened in two (dichade dioichthentes), they are revealed to contain statues of gods (agalmata theōn) inside. The craft analogy maps the artifact type (Silenus figurine with hidden divine interior) onto Socrates (outwardly ugly/satyric, inwardly divine)."

The noun silēnois names the crafted objects; the location hermoglypheiois specifies the workshop setting; dēmiourgoi identifies the craftsmen; the phrase dichade dioichthentes phainontai endothen agalmata echontes theōn specifies the hinge mechanism and hidden contents. The mapping from crafted object to person is explicit in the comparative construction."
limits: "This observation records the craft analogy structure within 215a-215b. It does not assess whether the analogy accurately characterizes Socrates, whether the hidden-god-inside image has philosophical or religious significance, or whether the analogy anticipates later claims about Socrates's nature."
review_status: accepted

The characterization maps Socrates onto the mythic satyr type: satyr-like appearance, hubristic behavior, and musical/verbal power surpassing Marsyas."
textual_basis: "At 215b, the text reads: 'I also say he resembles the satyr Marsyas. That you are like these in form (to ge eidos homoios ei toutois), not even you yourself would dispute.' The comparison to Marsyas adds a specific mythic figure to the general Silenus type; the three questions (hubristēs, auletēs) characterize Socrates's behavior and skill."
limits: "This observation records the Marsyas comparison and the hubristēs/auletēs characterization within 215b. It does not assess whether the hubristēs charge is justified, whether the comparison to Marsyas carries specific mythological content about Marsyas's fate, or whether the auletēs claim is meant literally or metaphorically."

observation: "At 215d, Alcibiades states that if he were not going to seem completely drunk, he would swear an oath to the company about what he has suffered from Socrates's speeches and continues to suffer. The claim simultaneously asserts the intensity of the experience (warranting an oath) and hedges the testimony by citing the social risk of appearing drunk as the reason for not swearing."
textual_basis: "At 215d, Alcibiades is reported to have said: 'If I were not going to seem completely drunk (komidēi doxein methyein), I would have sworn (omosas an) to you what I have suffered (pepontha) from this man's speeches and still now suffer (paschō eti kai nyni).' The optative with an (omosas an) marks the counterfactual: the oath would be sworn but for the feared appearance of drunkenness. The paired perfect and present tenses mark the experience as ongoing."
limits: "This observation records the counterfactual oath formulation and the drunkenness hedge. It does not assess whether Alcibiades is actually drunk, whether the hedge is sincere or ironic, whether the stated suffering refers to specific content outside this span, or whether the oath would have been informative."
review_status: accepted
```

When he hears Pericles and other good orators, he considers them to speak well but experiences nothing of the sort—his soul is not disturbed nor angered at being slavishly disposed. But hearing Pericles and other good orators, I thought they spoke well, but I suffered nothing of the sort, nor was my soul disturbed nor did it feel indignant at being slavishly disposed.' observation: "At 216a, Alcibiades reports that Socrates forces him (anankazei) to agree about his own condition: that he is greatly deficient in himself and yet neglects himself while attending to Athenian affairs. He states that he blocks his ears and flees like from the Sirens to avoid growing old sitting beside Socrates. The passage reports both the forced agreement as an ongoing pattern and the flight response it produces."
textual_basis: "At 216a, the text reads: 'For he forces me to agree that, being myself greatly deficient, I yet neglect myself but do the Athenians' business. By force, then, blocking my ears as if from the Sirens, I go fleeing, so that I may not grow old sitting beside this man.' The verb anankazei (forces) with homologein (to agree) explicitly marks elenctic compulsion. The Siren comparison (hosper apo tōn Seirēnōn) provides a mythic parallel for the irresistible yet dangerous pull of Socrates's speech."
limits: "This observation records Alcibiades's reported experience of elenctic compulsion at 216a. It does not assess whether the agreement is genuine or coerced, whether the Siren comparison accurately maps to the Homeric episode, or whether the flight is motivated by genuine self-knowledge or by resistance to it."

I therefore run away and flee him, and when I see him, I feel shame at the things agreed.' observation: "At 217c-217d, Alcibiades narrates a four-stage escalation of his seduction attempt, each stage involving greater intimacy and each stage meeting resistance: (1) challenging Socrates to exercise and wrestle alone with no result; (2) inviting Socrates to dinner, who did not quickly agree but was eventually persuaded; (3) on the first dinner, letting Socrates depart out of shame when he wanted to leave; (4) on the next visit, conversing far into the night and forcing Socrates to stay. Each stage is marked by failure or partial success requiring a stronger tactic."
textual_basis: "At 217c, first stage: Alcibiades challenged Socrates to exercise and they exercised together often alone (synegymnazonto) but nothing came of it (ouden gar moi pleon en). Second stage: deciding to make a direct assault (epitheteon), Alcibiades invited Socrates to dinner; Socrates did not quickly agree (oude touto tachy hypekousen) but was persuaded after time (chronoi epeisthe). At 217d, third stage: Socrates wanted to leave after dining and Alcibiades let him go, ashamed (aischynomenos apheka auton). Fourth stage: plotting again (authis de epibouleusas), Alcibiades conversed far into the night (dielegomen aei porro ton nykton) and forced him to stay (prosenankasa auton menein). The escalation moves from athletic contact to co-dining to enforced overnight proximity."
limits: "This observation records the escalation structure reported by Alcibiades within 217c-217d. It does not assess historical accuracy, whether the resistance was genuine or performed, or how the narrative continues in subsequent spans."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_symposium_0200
source_work: Symposium
stephanus_span: 217e-218a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/symposium.txt
  stephanus_span: 217e-218a
  start_marker: 217e
  end_marker: 218a
  start_char: 94535
  end_char: 95385
  text_sha256: 3b1eee64b05cfc42f4e42539c71a2fd545b6e489bf912771da41711d9347faec
greek_terms:
  - δηχθέντος
  - ἔχεως
  - ἐχίδνης ἀγριώτερον
  - ἐν φιλοσοφίᾳ λόγων
  - καρδίαν
  - ψυχήν
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: craft_analogy
feature_id: feature_candidate_291
feature_label: wound_comparison_for_discourse_effect
observation: At 217e-218a, Alcibiades uses a sustained analogy comparing the effect of philosophical discourses to a viper bite. He states that just as a viper-bitten person will describe the experience only to the similarly bitten, since only they will understand and forgive extreme behavior from pain, so too he has been bitten — in heart or soul — by philosophical discourses that cling more fiercely than a viper and make him do and say anything.
textual_basis: At 217e, Alcibiades introduces the analogy of the viper-bitten person (to tou dēchthentos hypo tou echeōs pathos) who speaks only to the bitten (tois dedēgmenois), since only they will know and forgive (gnōsomenois te kai syngnōsomenois). At 218a, he applies the comparison directly: he has been bitten (dedēgmenos) by something more painful — in heart or soul (kardian ē psychēn), struck and bitten by philosophical discourses (hypo tōn en philosophiāi logōn), which cling more fiercely than a viper (echidnēs agriōteron). The source domain (snake venom, physical pain, restricted communication among initiates of the wound) maps onto the target domain (philosophical argument, psychic wound, restricted audience of fellow philosophers).
limits: This observation records the analogy as a textual phenomenon within the cited span. It does not assess whether the analogy is accurate, whether Alcibiades is being ironic or sincere, or whether this snake imagery echoes other Platonic passages.
review_status: accepted
---
```

observation: At 219c, Alcibiades addresses the symposiasts with a courtroom vocative, calling them "judges" (dikastai) of Socrates's contempt/arrogance (hyperēphania). He states that they are judges of Socrates's hyperēphania, explicitly framing the evaluative judgment of Socrates's behavior as a quasi-judicial proceeding conducted by the assembled company.
textual_basis: At 219c, Alcibiades says: ō andres dikastai; dikastai gar este tēs Sōkratous hyperēphania. The phrase ō andres dikastai is the standard Athenian courtroom address to jurors, and the explanatory gar clause explains why the symposiasts are being called judges.
limits: This observation records the explicit courtroom address and framing at 219c. It does not assess whether the judicial framing is serious or ironic, or whether Alcibiades is parodying legal procedure. It does not claim that the symposium has become an actual judicial proceeding.

observation: At 219c, Alcibiades preemptively asserts that Socrates will not call his account a lie after narrating his overnight attempt. He then swears a double oath by gods (ma theous) and by goddesses (ma theas) to attest to the truth of his claim about sleeping beside Socrates.
textual_basis: At 219c, Alcibiades says to Socrates directly: oude tauta au, ō Sōkrates, ereis hoti pseudomai (you will not even say that I am lying about these things). He then swears: eu gar iste ma theous, ma theas (for know well, by gods, by goddesses). The double oath formula (ma theous, ma theas) and the preemptive denial that Socrates will accuse him of falsehood precede the claim about the night.
limits: This observation records the rhetorical structure of the oath and preemptive denial at 219c. It does not assess whether the oath is binding, whether the claim is actually true, or whether Socrates would in fact call it a lie. It does not claim that the oath is legally or religiously valid.

```

textual_basis: "At 220b, the text states that during a most terrible frost (pagos oiou deinotatou), all either did not go out or, if they did, were clothed amazingly (thaumasta hosa), shod and with feet wrapped in felt (pílous) and sheepskins (arnakidas). Socrates went out with a himation of the sort he used to wear before, barefoot (anypodētos) through the ice (krystallou), and walked more easily (rhaion) than the others who were shod (hypodedemenoi)."
limits: "This observation records the contrast Alcibiades draws between Socrates's barefoot comfort and the bundled others. It does not assess whether the account is historically accurate, whether the frost was exceptional, or whether the endurance has philosophical significance beyond the prosopographical detail."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_symposium_0227

greek_terms:

  - παλαιῶν
  - νῦν ὄντων
  - ἄξιον παντὸς θαύματος
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: prosopography
feature_id: feature_candidate_308
feature_label: socratic_incomparability_claim
observation: At 221c, Alcibiades states that although many wondrous things could be praised about Socrates, the fact that Socrates is like no human being — neither among the ancients nor among those now living — is worthy of all wonder. He supports this by noting that what Achilles was like one could compare to Brasidas and others, and what Pericles was like one could compare to Nestor and Antenor, implying that those figures have matches while Socrates does not.
textual_basis: At 221c, the text reads: 'Many other wondrous things one could praise about Socrates; but concerning other practices, one might perhaps say such things about another man as well. But that he is like no human being (mēdeni anthrōpōn homoion), neither of the ancients nor of those now living — this is worthy of all wonder (touto axion pantos thaumatos). For what sort Achilles was, one might compare Brasidas and others; and what sort Pericles was, Nestor and Antenor — and there are others too.' The incomparability claim is explicit and the paired historical exempla function as supporting contrast.
limits: This observation records the explicit textual claim at 221c. It does not assess whether the claim is historically accurate, whether the paired comparisons (Achilles/Brasidas, Pericles/Nestor/Antenor) are apt, or whether this reflects Alcibiades's genuine assessment versus a rhetorical climax within his encomium.
review_status: accepted
```

observation: At 221c, Alcibiades uses a rhetorical structure in which pairs of comparably great historical and mythic figures are matched with each other — Achilles with Brasidas, Pericles with Nestor and Antenor — to establish that other exceptional men can be assimilated to recognizable types, in contrast with Socrates who cannot be matched to any human predecessor or contemporary.
textual_basis: At 221c, the text reads: 'For what sort Achilles was, one might compare (apeikaseien) Brasidas and others; and what sort Pericles was, Nestor and Antenor — and there are others too.' The optative verb apeikaseien marks the comparison as possible and fitting. The figures are drawn from two domains: Achilles (epic hero) matched with Brasidas (Spartan general), Pericles (Athenian statesman) matched with Nestor and Antenor (Trojan elders from epic). The structure implies that all such figures fall into comparable categories unlike Socrates.
limits: This observation records the paired-exempla rhetorical structure at 221c. It does not assess whether the comparisons are historically accurate, whether Brasidas is a suitable match for Achilles, or whether the pairing implies specific evaluative judgments about the figures named.
review_status: accepted

limits: This observation records the explicit verbal-satyr-skin image applied to Socrates's speeches at 221e. It does not assess whether the characterization is accurate, whether the speeches actually contain valuable content inside, or whether this description applies to all of Socrates's speeches. textual_basis: "At 222a, the text states that when someone sees Socrates's speeches opened and gets inside them, he will find them having nous alone inside, then most divine and containing the most statues of virtue, and extending to everything fitting for someone to examine who intends to become beautiful and good."
limits: "This observation records the description of the inner content of Socrates's speeches as stated at 222a. It does not assess whether this characterization is accurate, whether it is consistent with the outer-layer characterization preceding this passage, or whether the kalos kagathos phrase carries specific philosophical weight."
review_status: accepted
---
```

feature_label: alcibiades_speech_reframed
observation: Socrates responds to Alcibiades's speech by declaring that Alcibiades seems sober, not drunk. The rationale is that Alcibiades would never have so cleverly concealed, by speaking in a circle, the true purpose of everything he said — placing the crucial point at the end as if it were a side-remark. Socrates explicitly rejects Alcibiades's self-presentation as a drunken truth-teller.
textual_basis: At 222c, Socrates says: 'You seem to me to be sober. For you would never have so cleverly concealed by speaking in a circle the purpose for which you said all these things, placing it at the end as if in a side-remark, as though you hadn't said everything for this purpose.' The verb Nēphein (to be sober) and the adverb kompsōs (cleverly/skillfully) with the phrase kyklōi periballomenos (speaking in a circle/indirectly) characterize the speech as deliberate and artful rather than impulsive.
limits: This observation records Socrates's explicit claim at 222c. It does not assess whether Alcibiades is actually drunk or sober, whether Socrates's interpretation is correct, or whether Alcibiades's speech was indeed a calculated rhetorical performance. It does not specify the purpose Socrates names, which continues in the text beyond this span.
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "Socrates refuses Alcibiades's request to put Agathon between them, citing a sympotic turn-order rule: since Alcibiades praised Socrates, Socrates must praise the one on his right (Agathon). If Agathon moves to recline under Alcibiades, the praise-order would be disrupted because Agathon would praise Socrates again before being praised by Socrates."
textual_basis: "At 222e, Socrates says: 'That's impossible. For you have praised me, and I in turn must praise the one on the right. If Agathon reclines under you, he will surely praise me again before being praised by me.' The reasoning links seating position to the order of encomiastic obligations: the right-side recliner receives praise from the left-side praiser."
limits: "This observation records Socrates's stated rationale for refusing the seating change. It does not assess whether this praise-order rule is a recognized sympotic convention or an ad hoc invention by Socrates. It does not claim that this rationale was the actual or sole reason for the refusal."
review_status: accepted
```

limits: "This observation records the komast invasion and dissolution as reported within 223b.
